ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u5	BUEMI (Aut (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT)-PSI-MAIE). Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to start the sitting with a greeting and appreciation. The greeting is to President Guido Raimondi, who will lead - the first Italian for over thirty-five years - the European Court of Human Rights, following the election took place in recent days. [[Applause from the groups (Aut (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT) - PSI-MAIE) and PD]] The recognition with which his colleagues thanked him is certainly a certificate of professionalism and authority of the person, but it is also a very important enunciation of the centrality of Italian legal culture in the contemporary development of international law. From our Peninsula comes not only one of the greatest burdens for the Court (the litigation is unfortunately high): we also express an inexhaustible capital of intellectual refinement, deep competence and self-dedication, of which Judge Raimondi is crystal clear expression. New challenges and heavy clouds are gathering on the future of the European Court: a parish vision is overwhelming one of the founding states, Great Britain, which considered the European Court precious to safeguard the freedom of the Continent, like the Queen's jewels. We must not regress, but rather re-launch the role of the Court in defending the rights and freedoms of all, first of all by making rapid ratification of its latest protocols. But above all, the accession of the European Union to the European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms (ECHR) as agreed in Lisbon must be made as soon as possible. After the impasse of the hostile opinion expressed by the Court of Luxembourg, here in the Senate a proposal was made for the opening of negotiations, focusing on the partial merger of the two courts, as is apparent in motion 1-00383. Whether this or otherwise is the preferred way, we must do so quickly, in order to prevent the flag of human rights being lowered when there is, more than ever, an urgent need for it. I am sure that the election of Judge Raimondi as President - in addition to confirming the excellent level of European jurisprudence, of which he has been a fundamental part in recent years - will give new impetus to this elaboration and I hope that our diplomacy will create the conditions to support it in all international and supranational fora.

And that's what scares you, that we can become a majority. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u16	Mr President, representatives of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, the need to reform Part II of the Constitution was born more than thirty years ago. All the attempts at constitutional revision carried out so far have not produced good results, therefore, the fundamental Charter of the Italian order still maintains intact all the elements outlined by the constituent Assembly in 1946-47. The only exception is the reform of Title V of 2001 of the center-left, which, indeed, was not an improvement reform at all but has worsened, and much, the structure of the Regions, Provinces and Municipalities. First of all, it must be made clear that constitutional laws, as well as constitutional revision laws, the discipline of which is found in Article 138 of the Italian Constitution, are the exclusive competence of Parliament, to which legislative power is attributed. The theory of the separation of the powers of Montesquieu, crystallized in our Constitutional Charter, excludes from the legislative process any interference of the executive power, attributed to the Government, and of the judicial power, entrusted to the judiciary. It is understandable why it is Parliament that has to legislate and therefore establish the lines within which the internal system is implemented, because it is the only institutional body legitimated to exercise popular sovereignty. Clearly, it is without prejudice to the wider and more legitimate right of criticism that must be exercised by anyone - and we would miss something else - only that the interference that we are seeing these days is intolerable, because they are interference from the flavor of the threat that a power outside the process allows to exert on the legitimate one. So today the theory of Montesquieu, so dear to us, no longer coincides with practice. It is now before everyone's eyes that the President of the Council, at the head of the executive power, said, arbitrarily and illegitimately, rhythms and times of the legislative process of constitutional reform. For me, and for many of my fellow Members in this House who have a deep respect for institutional prerogatives, this attitude, incomprehensiblely nothing at all opposed, is absolutely unacceptable. From the presence of the President-in-Office of the Council in this Chamber during his work, it is felt that the Premier is interpreting, I would say, in an overflowing way, the functions that have been assigned to him, almost to be the controller of Parliament. This is a question of institutional decency: the President of the Council should not always be so present. Instead, the story in recent months tells exactly the opposite. It was the beginning of February of this year when our President-in-Office of the Council entered the Chamber of Montecitorio at night and, wandering between the benches of the Members, required the Members of his party to approve both the constitutional reform he wanted that the time of realization, intimidating his own that, if they had not adapted, he would resign with consequent political elections, thus putting at risk the re-election of the members of the internal minority of the PD. Imagine what the many sided feathers of this country would have written (not to mention the foreign press) if President Berlusconi had been in the Chamber at night during the discussion and vote on constitutional reforms. [[Applause from Senator Rizzotti]] But you know, the rules, for the left, apply only and exclusively to others. But let us return to the merit of constitutional reform. First of all, the 1st Committee on Constitutional Affairs, whose seat was only an opportunity to declare the proposed amendments to Article 2 of the reform inadmissible, which has a significant impact on Article 57 of the Constitution, in the matter of the composition and election of the Senate of the Republic. This denies the parliamentary debate on fundamental points such as Article 2 and, in particular, on the election of members of the new Senate, we are here in this House to express our views and reflections on the subject which, unfortunately and reluctantly, we know that they will not have any echoes, since the reform game is being played outside this House, where the greatest commitment is not so much in trying to conclude a broad agreement on the content, but it is in carrying out the buying campaign in order to have sufficient numbers and necessary for the approval of the reform, forgetting that these are changes to the Italian Constitution, which represents the document that is the basis of the democratic coexistence of the country and, therefore, any possible modification of it would need the maximum consensus among all political forces. Here the line is defined in the direction of PD and spread via Twitter. The critical profiles of this constitutional revision bill are many and concern; the multiplication of legislative procedures, which can give rise to a real legislative babel; the imbalance between the House and the Senate in the election of the guarantee bodies, beginning with the President of the Republic; the division of competences between the State and the Regions, apparently simplified, but in reality even more complicated and less functional. Each of these aspects deserves a wide and articulated discussion, but it is enough to recall the many criticisms contained in the 80 pages written by the Department of Studies of Palazzo Madama. A rich dossier in which senate technicians point by point criticize the text of constitutional reform armored by Prime Minister Renzi and Minister Boschi. The most important doubts concern the competences of the new Senate, further reduced after the changes made to the House. Basically, for the technicians of Palazzo Madama the Senate would be reduced to an almost useless organ, so much so that they write: The changes made in the first reading in the other branch of the Parliament seem to provide for the Senate to perform functions only in conjunction with the Chamber of Deputies. They do not appear to be provided for functions performed by the Senate alone, nor, it would be said, functions exercised by the Senate independently of the Chamber. Once the function of "exclusively" maintaining relations with the European Commission has disappeared, the question arises: what exactly would this new Senate do? Another point: as it is written, the article that entrusts only to the House the vote on the declaration of war is almost an exception in Europe and it is a unicum if we consider the European countries that have a bicameral parliament. Instead, Ireland, Poland and Slovenia provide that the consent to the declaration of war is made only by the House of Deputies, which, however, in each country is elected with a proportional system. And then, to close my speech, I borrow some verses of a song by a songwriter very dear to the left, Roberto Vecchioni, who in his famous song says: And the greatest conquered Nation after Nation, and when it was in front of the sea there was a c... (I leave it to you to complete the rhyme), for nothing could be conquered further on; and so much way to see a desperate sun, and always equal and always as when it had departed. Here, President Renzi, for the good of the Italians you are not also in front of the sea and avoid, especially the Italians, a awakening with a desperate sun. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII. Congratulations.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u17	Senator Girotto is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u18	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, first of all I note that, compared to 15 July 2014, when this draft law was examined at first reading, the text certainly does not seem to be changed for the better. Many reasons explain this opinion, but, in this respect, the basis for my speech was given to me by the statements made by the President of the Council two days ago at the PD management meeting. In this meeting, Renzi, for at least twice, claimed a preference for a Senate representation entrusted only to the mayors and therefore with total exclusion of representatives of the Regions. Now, one might ask the following question: can our Premier, who has a degree in law, even if he has been described as a little expert by the Court of Auditors, really escape that in our legal system it is the Regions, and not the Municipalities, which are responsible for the legislative power? That legislative power is the most important among public functions and, not by chance, is entrusted to elected representatives of the people? What is perfectly logical that an Assembly which intervenes in the exercise of its legislative function, especially on matters affecting regional autonomy, should see among its members the entities representing the Regions? The President of the Regions will not open his mouth, we are sure, but we are not interested in this aspect. We note, however, that if the President-in-Office does not understand the above, he certainly does not come out of it very well. We believe, however, that Renzi is not totally fasting constitutional law, but that he simply does not care about the effectiveness and coherence of the reform, being interested simply in presenting a new medal to the Italians. We do not doubt that, if the reform is well received, it will be thanks to you, but that any defects will certainly be attributable to Parliament. On the other hand, had our Premier not proposed that more than twenty senators should be appointed by the President of the Republic? That's absurd. What wonders, then? It doesn't take much to understand it: in a moment of disaffection for politics, Renzi, instead of trying to seriously cure its causes, chose a target on which to focus the general impopolity and identified it in the Regions. The Regions, it seems, are responsible for all the disasters of the Italian State; it is the competing power of the Regions the cause of all evil, forgetting or pretending to ignore all the jurisprudence of the Constitutional Court of clearly anti-regionalist direction, which for example leads back to the coordination of public finance by now any state discipline, compressing precisely the regional legislative power. It does not pay to have practically destroyed the regional legislative power, this majority - if that really will be - has well thought, in the passage of the bill to the House, to further reduce the functions of the Senate, which now on many aspects are further reduced. The Senate is limited to contributing to the link between the State and territorial authorities, to contributing to the link between territorial authorities and the European Union and to contributing to the evaluation of public policies. These functions were certainly not easily defined, in part new, but it is evident the further marginalization of the Senate, functional, moreover, to its nature as representative body of self-governments that they want to suppress. Why, then, keep a body that does nothing and risks becoming an assembly of politicians, excuse the term, trumpeted at the elections of the House, which is offered a consolation prize, from which to take advantage, perhaps every other week, going to Rome? This is certainly not the senate to which our constituent Fathers had thought, nor is it true that the second Chambers in the other ordinances count for nothing, even without uncomfortableing the American Senate, which is not second. The Senate risks not counting anything in Italy: in this sense, the vexed question of the direct electivity of senators does not solve the problem, but could at least mitigate the imbalance of powers that is produced also thanks to the new electoral law for the House. We do not fall into misunderstandings and furberias: it is a silly argument to say that electoral law is something different from constitutional reform, when vice versa it is undeniable that electoral law is a decisive part of the structure of the party system, decisively affecting representation and therefore influencing the form of government of each country. It is not by chance that today, that is to say almost done, the inspirations of electoral reform pretend to realize that perhaps, with this system, it will be too easy for a majority even not high to elect a President of the Republic (read Professor D'Alimonte's speech on "Il Sole 24 Ore" last Sunday), all things that we - theoretically the less experienced - had long highlighted and deplored. We do not talk about the incredible confusion that will be created in the system of sources, with the presence of equal and non-equal bicameral laws, with matters of competence entrusted to agreements, perhaps opaque, between Presidents of the Chamber and Senate, and with possible and predictable litigation before the Constitutional Court actuated by the Regions (or perhaps also by the State) in defense of their respective competences. It is even obvious that within such a broad reform, of course, there are also no negative elements: I am thinking of the possibility for a parliamentary minority to request a judgment from the Constitutional Court on electoral laws (although Article 13 of the draft seems ambiguous, it is unclear whether the judgment of the Court should cover the entire law and whether it should be considered exclusive, precluded a judge from turning again to the Court, perhaps after years, and while observing that the reference to electoral laws is likely to exclude all the legislation of outline, for example on the financing and management of electoral campaigns). Preventive intervention should avoid the legal contortions that the Court had to undertake in order to declare Porcellum unlawful. The point is that one, or some aspects that may be acceptable in the reform, does not exonerate from a global judgment, which can only be negative; but we did not decide to undertake a reform process with regard to a plurality of profiles and dishomogeneous institutes, forcing Parliament, and tomorrow the Italians, to say yes or no about everything, in bulk. Finally, let me say something about the question of emendability. We do not understand why Rule 104 of our Rules of Procedure can apply to a legislative procedure concerning constitutional revision; in twenty-six hearings of constitutionalists, as many as twenty spoke about the fact that it is absolutely possible to speak on Article 2. Moreover, there are specific precedents: during the debate on Article 68 of the Charter, in 1993, for example, the then President of the Camera Giorgio Napolitano admitted the emendability of a rule that had already been voted in accordance, both in Montecitorio and in Palazzo Madama. That's about merit. Let us now turn to the method, the true insinable question, the true original sin that I personally do not forgive you, but I assume that history will not forgive you too. The true original sin consists in the fact that you are acting having a merely formal legitimacy, but absolutely not ethical. I am referring to the fact that the Porcellum has been declared unconstitutional and that there is a majority prize that allows you to do what you are doing and that is to do an abuse of dominant position. I do not know how many of you work in the field of trade or industry, in any case the abuse of a dominant position is a very serious practice. You are in an illegitimate majority position. You are not respecting a precise indication from the Constitutional Court that this Parliament should have confined itself to the management of ordinary administration, to adopting a new electoral law and then going immediately to new elections. That is the point: you are not legitimate; you are not respecting the Constitutional Court and, therefore, the Italians and this is unforgivable. Absolutely unforgivable. You are abusing a position in which you have been placed by an illegitimate rule. Let us not hide behind a finger: citizenship does not call for this reform. Citizenship calls for well-being, which largely results from work and the labour sector today is in difficulty because it lacks stability, lacks coherence in the rules that are the result of the influence exerted by lobbying. Making this Senate less autonomous and less powerful will change nothing: the influence of the lobby will not change and we will find ourselves at the same point even after. Nothing will change at all, I say it to future memory of the Italians. This reform will not change policy. As long as it is influenced by lobbyists, it will not change. We believe that such a broad constitutional change, involving over forty articles, must be launched by a constituent assembly elected with a proportional system that would present two advantages: that of being representative of Italian society in its entirety and that, above all, of being free from the constraints of the government's activity. Article 138 was introduced in order to make some adjustments, certainly not to implement such a far-reaching reform. To be clear, to limit the danger of a government that would turn the Constitution upside down for its own use and consumption, by majority blows, someone had already thought about it and had gone to the shelter with a bill that would have been most appropriate. I am talking about a bill signed by 65 Members of Parliament (including Napolitano and Mattarella), which was at the heart of protecting our democracy and which, if it had been adopted, would simply not allow you to do what you are doing. Perhaps it is true: this is Renzi's reform - only Renzi's - and not the Italians and it would be advisable to distance themselves before it is too late. [[Applause from the M5S Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u19	Senator Cervellini is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u20	Mr President, representatives of the Government, senators and senators, we are discussing the reform of the Constitution of the Italian Republic. Instead of a solemn and profound confrontation, everything is marked by Renzi's hectic agenda: reforms - therefore - at any cost, regardless of their specific weight in terms of institutional, economic, social and ethical value. Renzi's agenda has no time, no desire, no thickness to go beyond tweets, it's all it's looking for. Better then complete the new reading at the Senate before it triggers the budget session in mid-October. This again emerges the fearful legislative futurism, quoted on several occasions, which claims that this Government is constantly looking for models that increase the speed of law approval in a decision-making without any real decision. The withdrawal of the amendments was not enough either: the Democratic Party has decided to run even faster, forcing us to yet another marathon in the House and demeaning once again the weight of the work in the Commission. But we've gotten used to it. Yet there is a time of incubation to mature thoughtful ideas and solutions, since we are talking about substantial changes to our Constitution and our democracy, to which the constituent Fathers have reserved appropriate time. It really has never been seen - at least in a liberal and democratic country - a government that is so heavy on the substance of a constitutional law. Never! Yet this Executive has invested itself in the right to change forty articles of the Constitution without a parliamentary mandate. This happens precisely in our own system, which has an open Constitution capable of guiding fundamental political choices, and a tradition of constitutionalists who have been able to build a system based on weights and counterweights able to withstand the test of time and profoundly different contexts. Today we are engaged in a bill that is unable even to overcome the perfect bicameralism harmoniously. However, the real danger - we have said so many times - lies in the combination between the reform of the Senate and Italicum, which risks undermining the very foundations of democracy with a majority that will have a free hand to the House and the Senate, with the power to determine all institutional structures (from the President of the Republic, to the Supreme Council of the judiciary). We are turning towards an encircled presidentialism, without even the dignity of what refers to the European and American tradition (of the North, at least, because perhaps we cannot refer to the South American countries as well). We are moving forward with solutions which are all strictly extra-parliamentary, some of which - I recognise this - are even imaginative. I am thinking of several amendments, of the conjunction with the election of regional councils and of the emergence, between the two extremes of the election of second degree and direct election, of the hypothesis of the blocked sliding list, which would have allowed the citizen to compete in the choice of regional councillors destined to enter the new Senate of the hundred. All in order to avoid the supreme evil, that is to say the vote by the citizens. I recall that universal suffrage is not in our readiness, but is part of the founding values of democracy, forming part of the foundation on which a democratic system is based. If possible, then, we try to bypass also the confrontation with minorities and we make our way and emerge a real crisis of abstinence from a vote of confidence also with respect to the Constitution (I am not inventing it, as we read it in the newspapers). Moreover, many of you do not see any difference from what happened a few months ago on the provision on school, on jobs act or on Italicum. It is no coincidence that today, with the White Night for the school, throughout Italy, and in Rome, thousands and thousands of teachers of students, parents, will manifest against the Good School. And in Rome it is no coincidence that, together with the initiatives in front of the Ministry of Viale Trastevere, initiatives will take place in front of the Senate. The Palaces may not have understood this, but awareness is being made in the citizens, women and men, who live and work in our Republic, that there is a thread that dramatically and negatively unites the action carried out by the Renzi Government in recent months. Another doubt grabs the prospects for revision of Title V of Part II of the Constitution. Decentralisation or centralization? On this binomial is played the great misunderstanding of the reform. According to the government's orientation in its accompanying report of 8 April 2014, the reform intends to enhance, by declining it in a new way, institutional pluralism and the autonomous principle, with the aim of increasing the rate of democracy of our system, in the awareness that the autonomy of bodies other than the State constitutes an irreplaceable element of enrichment of the institutional system. The closer public power is to the citizens, the higher the quality of democratic life according to the principle of vertical subsidiarity, which is also incorporated into the institutional architecture of the European Union. Today it is therefore a question of giving impetus to a process that really guarantees local autonomy a virtuous involvement in the decision-making circuit at national level. However, if we look at the reform, both in the original version and in the recent version, reporting the changes in reading to the House, there is a clear deviation from the initial objectives. In view of the reduction of regional autonomy, especially on the legislative authority side, the new Senate is absolutely inadequate to satisfy the right balance between competence and participation, i.e. between the guarantee of the political direction of the regions within the national political circuit. Without prejudice to our political position of opposition to a Senate of nominations that is not of an elective nature, it is evident that both from the structural point of view (with position, numerical distribution and mode of election) and from the functional one, the powers are many from the quantitative point of view, but of little incisiveness, especially in the relationship between the State and the regions. The new Senate does not present itself at all as the Chamber of Regions, despite the latter being the expression that would be introduced in the new Article 55, with the Regions no longer being political-legislative bodies but oriented to the administration and coordination of local administrations in the territorial dimension of the so-called vast area. To this we add the ruin, as happened with the fake abolition of the provinces. In fact, democracy and the participation of those instruments that, on the other hand, produced precious democracy, participation and acts of good governance have been abolished only and exclusively. In fact, according to this plan, the keys to all public policies are to be returned to the State, to remove the list of subjects of concurrent legislation and to redefine the system of repartition of the legislative power between the State and the regions on empty lists of subjects, with the umpteenth operation of facade, yet another incoherence. I repeat once again that the Left Ecology and Freedom has proposed a differentiated bicameralism, for functions, in which the Parliament legifers through few and qualified laws of principle and controls the government's work in the implementation, while the Chamber of Deputies, through the trust link with the Government, should deal with the implementation of the political direction. The Senate should be set up as a Chamber responsible for the protection of rights and guarantees, capable of promoting quality legislation, assessing the impact, even territorial, of public policies and exercising a penetrating inspection and control power: for example, on the work of the leaders of public structures and public-sector companies. The zeroing of any balance of powers, or the prospect of a hypertrophic executive, is therefore not acceptable. Is there a majority of Italians, with reference to the absolute votes of 2013, who freely voted this proposal? The pact of the Nazarene has skipped, as far as I am concerned, which, however, answered the question I am asking, we have a hyper-majoritarian system that is proposed by representatives who are the result of the unconstitutional Porcellum. In fact, in the House, the Democratic Party has enjoyed the majority prize by virtue of the coalition Italia Bene Comune, that is, with that contribution - albeit limited - that made the difference, brought by Left Ecology and Freedom. In the Senate, with a different electoral system, in the same way, without that contribution there would have been no majority prize to the Democratic Party, for example, in Piedmont and Lazio. If this assumption is true (and it is absolutely true because it is confirmed by incontrovertible data, numbers and certificates, not polls or exit polls), I would also invite information to be careful when talking about majority: majority of what? The elected, not the votes of the citizens. And in the referendum - plebiscite that will conclude this path, other than plebiscite, the referendum will mark an unprecedented element, it will be the first simulation on the field of Italicum, in the real country, not in vitro. If a majority is the result of an electoral system and not of a real and proportional consensus it does not exercise wisdom and balance, it cannot claim that a real majority, reduced to a minority by an unconstitutional law, is more realistic than the king. All opposition, as is right, will unite against these evil proposals. [[Applause from the Misso-SEL Group]]

[[Applause from the M5S Group]] Evidently you feel the already sewn role of the one in this country who will decide for everyone. And we, dear citizens, ask ourselves when we can feel free. In my opinion, we are free when we are not subject to the opinion and power of men, who if they want can oppress us: being free does not mean having a good master, but not having masters. Today we are talking about constitutional reform. Starting from the meaning of the words, which are particularly important in politics, we read that the word reform indicates a radical change for which a social institution takes on new form; a thing is born or reborn very different from that which previously existed. Reforming is changing, for the sake of improvement, a state of affairs or an institution. The constitutional revision is another thing. It is the change, the revision of some details, but it is not the birth of a new Constitution. Instead, the reformers of this legislature speak explicitly of constitutional reform. Minister Boschi and the President-in-Office of the Council speak explicitly of constitutional reform; indeed, they use tones of great emphasis as the mother of the political and civil battles we are facing. The Constituents had another idea. When they discussed Article 138, they were talking about constitutional revision. We were talking about the modification of one, at most two articles. Here we are talking about total debarking. But Article 138 authorizes the revision of the Constitution, it does not authorize the reform of the Constitution. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] No parliament, much less that elected on the basis of an unconstitutional electoral law, has or must have the authority to reform the Constitution, with the obvious consideration that those who do so are doing so for their own sake. Then there is another point that deserves reflection. The act of rewriting a Constitution is par excellence the act of sovereign power. So, reformers, in this country, sovereign power still belongs to the people and therefore you cannot change the Constitution, also because no one has elected you to do so. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] To read also that Renzi, not elected by anyone, even impels himself to threaten the second office of the State with the affirmation that if he opens to changes to Article two it is necessary to convene Parliament immediately makes the idea of the standing and danger of this arrogant character. Renzi says that Italians have been waiting for this reform for seventy years. We wonder why he wants to reform the Constitution so quickly. Because it wants to set up as soon as possible a Senate of nominations, which will enjoy parliamentary immunity, because it wants to impose an electoral law that will see in the future a Parliament with three quarters of nominations, which in turn will have decision-making power over the appointment of the Constitutional Court, the President of the Republic and the Supreme Council of the judiciary. Their superexpert professors, such as Quagliariello, in the work begun in the Committee of Wise Men explained that the country has been waiting too long for this constitutional reform. Quagliariello, however, forgot to say that in 2006 the country had already rejected, through a referendum, the reform proposed by the then government. So we do not see this spasmodic expectation, this suffering and this trauma of the Italians to have this constitutional reform. But Quagliariello gives us the answer to this by saying that there is global competition and that we have inadequate institutions. It claims that the other countries of Western democracies have been able to face global competition by reforming the institutions and we have not. False. The Western countries have not changed their Constitutions, they have reformed the governments of those who govern, they have changed economic policies and that is how they have overcome the crisis. They haven't changed their constitutions. But then we have a doubt. Will it not be that the problem of the economic crisis, of this crisis that consumes us, that corrodes us, that empties us, that takes away the hope, that insecurizes us, that impoverishes us, that makes the young people flee from our country perhaps does not derive from the slowness of our Constitution, but from political corruption, tax evasion and your frightening arrogance and incompetence? Is that not the cause? The bicameral system is considered to be cumbersome and slow: this is another firm point of your statements, but it is false! Well, have you passed a law every six days, considering also Saturday, Sundays and holidays in August and Christmas and this would be slow? Article 54 of the Constitution states that all citizens have a duty to be faithful to the Republic and to observe the institutions and laws; but if, instead of being faithful to republican laws, I am faithful to a man, I am already outside the duties of a citizen. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u24	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, in 2014 I did not take part in the final vote on the Boschi bill; along with me, there were thirteen other senators from the PD Group. It was a painful and painful choice, like all those who make themselves stand out from their companions. Last year I had appreciated the work of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs and the rapporteurs (Senator Finocchiaro and you, President Calderoli), in particular on the definition of the competences that the new Senate should have. But other nodal points in that bill didn't convince me. I remember some of them. I am referring first of all to the disruption of the balance, also numerical, between the House and the Senate, which went from a ratio of 1 to 2 to a ratio of 1 to 6,3. You're gonna tell me, what does it matter? It matters at the time when the two Chambers are recognized of the functions they must perform together, for example the election of the President of the Republic. Everything is concatenated; I was little convinced that the election of the President of the Republic took place in the manner in which, for now, it is defined in the Boschi bill. I was not convinced by the composition of the Senate, elected by the Regional Councils (as the bill says). It did not convince me that, in that context, the same principle of parliamentary immunity was extended to the regional senators, which is attributed to the elected directly by the people by universal suffrage, that is to say to the Members and, today, to the senators themselves. I wasn't convinced of other minor things. This is where that choice comes in. We are now called upon to discuss, and possibly amend, the text revised by the House. I must confess to you that, if this text were to remain identical in substance, I am afraid that I should turn that non-voting into a negative vote. But I trust that this will not happen. I hope, hope and believe that the conditions can be created for a vote in favour. I have to say that the stage of preparation for this debate, that is to say, for this general debate and then for what will be the examination and the approval or non-approval of the amendments, could have been better: as it has been, it has not benefited. Of course, the threat of millions of amendments may not have calmed the climate; it certainly has not calmed it. However, the decision to avoid going to the Commission could have been different even after these amendments had been withdrawn, taking into account the fact that on some important points there is a sensitivity in the public which is not perfectly aligned with the draft law that we have submitted at the moment, as is confirmed by recent surveys. I remember the last poll published by the Â"Corriere della sera", a newspaper that certainly at this stage cannot be called hostile to the Government: according to this poll, large majorities are for a Senate elected by direct universal suffrage and, at the same time, are in favour of constitutional reform going into effect. We therefore have in this House the need to improve the text that has been delivered to us by the House in order to bring the reform to fruition, not to block it. It has not helped the convergence between the different thoughts and the different sensibilities, which are quite different and - let me be honest - more noble than the search for the consent of the refugees. Emphasizing this reform as who knows what and saying that for seventy years this reform is expected by the Italians is clearly a forcing. In 1945 the war had just ended and the constituent assembly had not yet been elected, but that is not the point: we could more simply say that Italy has been trying to change its Constitution for about thirty years, evidently because it believes that the moment of representation, which has rewarded all the other needs just ended World War II, must give way to the principle and the need for governance. The problem is how much the step is given, what is the balance that must be established between the moment of representation and that of governability. Extremism is dangerous. We must strengthen the government's action - that is true - but, at the same time, we cannot cancel and reduce to a simulacrum the representation of the electoral body, which must be somehow preserved. I would add that the pressure that has been exerted on the Presidency of this House has not benefited either, which is my personal solidarity, without any indication, either formal or surreptitious, on what the Presidency's desirable decisions should be. Respect means commenting on decisions once made, not pressing for them to have one sign rather than another before they are taken. I come to the crucial points because, despite these difficulties, which I have always wanted to recall, there is the possibility of a wider convergence, of a convergence of thoughts and not of small personal interests. The last point in my speech concerns the Presidency of the Republic: it is a crucial step. It is important that this supreme judiciary remains the expression of national unity and not of a majority built through the prize provided by Italicum. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u26	Gentlemen, this constitutional bill you're carrying out is based on lies and betrayal, but in the new language you're carrying on, it would be better to say it's based on a disinformation campaign. It is true that the Constitution gives Parliament the privilege of being able to touch the Constitution itself, but since we are the representatives of the citizens, we should first ask the citizens, perhaps writing it in the electoral programme, if we happen to want, once we have entered Parliament, to go to touch the Constitution, and not by doing it "aumm aumm," finding different numbers from time to time in Parliament. That is at least incorrect, because the only pact that we really make with the voters is the following: this is the programme and this must be continued. Instead, the program becomes rag paper a second later. It wasn't in the PD program, it wasn't in Letta's program, it wasn't even in Renzi's program: Renzi invented it at the table with Napolitano and someone else thinking well. So you're traitors, and that's the first thing. The second thing is the lie: all the reasons you have put forward to carry forward this constitutional bill, which I will now go briefly to dismantle, are lies. For example, you have said that this constitutional bill serves to speed up the legislative process, so we need a preferential lane with this constitutional change. This is false, because such tools already exist. There is the decree-law, for example: when a decree-law arrives the Conference of the Leaders I put it before everything; and moreover you have the instrument of the vote of confidence. So the preferential lane is already there and you don't need to do what you declared need to do. Then you say that this constitutional bill stabilizes governments. At last you have learned not to call it "governability" but the stability of the Executive; but it is not the same, because history teaches that, even when there was a very large parliamentary majority, there can always be causes for the dismantling of the Government. Do you remember Fini, when he decided to abandon the PdL-Lega majority? We all know how it turned out. So you can't guarantee that either. You said another dance, indeed, Renzi said it, but you repeated it at megaphone; there is the software "PD" that tells you what to say: "Repeat what Renzi says." You said that the Constitution is sixty-seven years old and needs to be changed. The Constitution has already been changed fifteen times since 1963, with fifteen constitutional laws; in the last twenty years it has been changed ten times, so it is not true that it has never been changed. Besides, you are now going to touch Title V, which was changed in 2001 and which made disasters, as you were told. Before touching the Constitution, we must go and see the effects of a change, which take place in the long term, when the Constitutional Court begins to be concerned with the problem of saying what is good and what is not. The effects of what you want to do - this constitutional "cracker" - will perhaps manifest in twenty or thirty years and we will understand only after the damage you have done, of course if someone doesn't get to fix it (and hopefully it will be us). It is not even true, therefore, that the Constitution is always the same. Then you climbed on the soapy mirror looking for parallels with other nations that have nothing to do with us (and this comes from reading your speeches on shorthand reports). You said that we should compare the Italian system and take examples from the United Kingdom, France and Germany. Congratulations! The United Kingdom is a constitutional monarchy, France is a presidential Republic and Germany is a federal state; Italy is none of the three. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] You cannot, regardless of a cultural fact, go and buy completely different situations. Italy has a bicameral system that is so, that it is a rarity because our form of government is not the one that adopted others, that historically they have found themselves in a federal form (see Germany) or that they have a monarchy that has long been memorable (such as Great Britain). It's no use, then, that we go fishing for their Senate. Then you mentioned Germany - because you see that you like it, you have a kind of penis envy - saying that it is the closest model, where the Senate is not directly elected. Which is true, but there is a different electoral system there and that, of course, you have not said; you have also cleverly omitted that in the German system over 70 percent of the laws are examined in a situation of equal bicameralism, not as in the constitutional reform project, in which you plan to put a hundred people, elected or not elected (do you, so much does not make sense, because clearly this distinction is fake), to graze and you do not know well to do what. In fact, what the Senate will eventually be able to do is little or nothing, except drain resources and suck money (so much you don't pay and so it's okay anyway). Then you said what I think is the supreme ball: you need a change of Constitution to modernise the nation, which has already had, as we said, fifteen changes of Constitution. There is a nation that has the same Constitution, about, since 1789 and that turns two hundred and twenty-seven years old. I say the same Constitution because the core of this Constitution has never been changed. We are talking about the United States of America and some of you tell me that the United States of America is not a modern nation. They have a lot of flaws, but they're the most modern nation, so it's not true that the change of constitution modernises a nation. But let us look for a moment - because we must also learn from others, since you say that we must take an example - what is the mode of constitutional change in the United States of America and how it has developed over time. The mechanism is that of the amendment: the Constitution, as a parent nucleus, is not touched. This is the fundamental principle and the supreme law is not touched. In the United States so far there have been 27 changes in two hundred and twenty-seven years, only 27. What have these changes done? Did they remove or add? They have either taken power from the Commission or added civil rights. They abolished slavery, they put the vote to women, they restricted presidential mandates to two, they restricted the salaries of congressmen and senators: all of them amendments that extended the power of the people, because on their front page it says "We, the people," not "We," the people bound to what the Parliament decides, "the people is sovereign," period. The Constitution is therefore of the and for the people, not for the use and consumption of someone, in here, who comes and goes and does not know what he does. How are the changes made? This is also interesting, in fact: if you want to preserve a Constitution, you have to make it rigid in relation to the adventurer on duty. Firstly, we need a two-thirds majority both in Congress and in the Senate; in fact, 75% of the states must say yes, regardless of their popularity: a state like California, therefore, which has 38 million inhabitants, at the time of ratification, weighs exactly like the least populous state, which for the ignorant is Wyoming, which makes 500,000 inhabitants. This is in order to safeguard the principle that even the small State must have the same representation, a word absolutely unknown to you. There is more, however: you go in the sense of the centralization of power, that is, the President of the Republic must be yours, a choice of the majority (let it never be that others also get caught); and the Constitutional Court, poor, always comes after, to say that a certain law is unconstitutional. An example that always comes from that side, instead, works in another way: it is true that it is a system of common law and not jurisprudential like ours, but any law decays at the same time when it is used in a process. The Federal Courts, when there is a constitutional doubt, have the power to declare that unconstitutional law immediately, not as you do, that you make a pig like Porcellum or this Italicum or the Alfano law (and there would be an exterminated list of them!), laws that work for their own use and consumption, until, at a certain point, the Court arrives with pain and cuts them away, perhaps after a year, a year and a half, two, three or five years and more. Constitutional law, the Constitution, must be at the top of everything, not after: if it is to come first, everything that is at odds must stop. You don't have this. I also wanted to quote the fifth amendment to the U.S. Constitution, which I think is what you like most, because it allows the accused in a trial to shut up without incurring the crime of insulting the Court, because what he says could harm him. I'm sure you should put it in; On the contrary, you have already predicted that one can lie during the trial without being incriminated: in Italy, it is legitimate to lie in order to defend himself in a trial; in the United States this is unlawful. A nice fifth amendment in Italian sauce! Closed all these pleasantries, I observe the following: it has just been shown that what you would like to achieve without a reform affecting about fifty articles of the Constitution. When one realizes that excessive force is being put into constitutional change, there is a doubt that there is an underground design: we have the certainty of it; we have not yet understood it, but this is definitely there. There is no need for speed, because there is already. Do you have the problem of double trust? You can copy this on our proposal: take away the confidence of one of the two Chambers, that is, do not nail one of the two branches of Parliament to have to have a majority, so perhaps extend the parliamentary guarantee; in this way, the Members of the other House do not have the bond of majority and can find agreements in a free way on points that can be shared. What were once called variable majorities is not a wrong thing, they are the freedom of the parliamentarians, while a system like what you're drawing cage freedom, so much so that the parliamentarians become free only when there is the secret vote: incredible, isn't it? It must be done "aumm aumm": we saw it when there is the secret vow, that the majorities crumble, because you keep them with an iron fist. I have heard the speeches of several people among you who, with their trembling voice, are terrified to say that extra word which determines sanctions expulsions or transfers within the party. So you don't have freedom, and with that you definitely don't recover it. Last thing: I have not heard any of you constitutionalists say what should really be done, wanting to touch the Constitution, to expand the foundation of the Republic, that is to say, being a parliamentary Republic, a representative democracy and not a presidential system. How can we increase Parliament's prerogatives? This is a question to which you have not answered, or rather, you have done so by saying: "Increasing the strength of the Government." Bravo, that's exactly the opposite. Or, how do you actually make the people sovereign? By increasing the means of direct democracy: whenever you can - and you can practically always - you go to the citizen and ask him: What do you think? Don't tell me that it can't be done or that this isn't an evolved country thing, because Switzerland does it: people vote at the post office with a badge and don't tell us that this is a complicated system. But you have also reduced these spaces by compressing the methods and systems of direct democracy that the Constitution also provides for. But again, the possibility of expanding the faculties of local authorities - since you say that the Senate should represent these "fake" autonomy - means going to touch Title V that you have already touched with the supremacy clause, that is, attributing more power to the center. You might as well get rid of these wheelchairs called Regions if you don't recognize any power in the end. Moreover, there is a constitutional jurisprudence that helps to understand now how to modify Title V by eliminating all the clauses of unconstitutionality due to competition between the laws of the State and the regional laws; but also this is an unknown one. You have punctuated yourself with your fake minority of the Democratic Party Group on the electivity of the Senate: a game of the parties. Don't tell me your problem is that senators are not elected. What's the point of electing people who don't do anything? Delete this system. Senator Fabbri then said that there are too many MPs compared to the number of people and brought China into play, which has 1,300 million inhabitants. You don't know that and so I tell you: the smaller the population, the more this relationship changes. Portugal has a population of 10 million against our 60 million, but it does not have a sixth of our Members: it has two hundred, that is a third. If the population were 1000 people, using the proportionality that you brought into play, the number of representatives should be zero. The Senate has a budget of about EUR 550 million, or just over half a billion. The 550 million includes the costs of the palaces, the vitality of former senators who do not want to abolish, the pensions of former employees, as well as the salaries of current employees and Minister Boschi know this well, since some parliamentary advisers, currently located at his or other ministerial staff, are paid directly by the Senate. Speaking of employees, you have provided for in the transitional rules the unique role of the employees of the two chambers but, after the judgments of internal justice - we apply domestic justice here - on appeals for salary ceilings, there is no longer the same economic treatment as employees. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u47	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, in this House we are not only discussing the reform of the Senate but also the rule of closing down a new institutional structure and the advent of a new political system. I say this not to evoke scenarios from the last days of Pompeii, nor to dramatize this passage as if the approval of the reform, which I hope, should be the signal able to unleash hell or paradise. I say this, much more simply - and I would say serenely if by now the "being serene" had not assumed sinister connotations - so that the scope of the passage that we are facing is clear. (at 12.11 a.m.) (Followed by Mr Quagliarello). We are at a crucial junction, of the legislature and not only. From the day the reform process is completed, nothing will be the same as before. We will have a new constitutional scaffolding but we will also have, in conjunction with it, a new electoral system. We will have a set-up and we will also be able to vote again, an option which has so far not been formally foreclosed but obstructed in substance by the random nature of a transitional system. That is why I would like to devote the time that I am allowed to today but also to tomorrow and, following Aldo Moro's admonition, to a next day the day after tomorrow. I give you the many reasons why we have supported and supported this reform and we will continue to make every effort to improve it. It is not a shot in the night but the result of a work matured throughout this legislature, from the Commission established by President Napolitano to the Letta Government, up to the often obscure but decisive work that we have assured on aspects perhaps less media but of enormous importance as those underlying the new Title V. We could, and could, even boast a birthright on the possible solution for the election of future senators. This argument, which in my opinion and I said it in time, had to be "seated." It is not in fact from the system of election of its members that derive the character and prerogatives of the Senate. On the contrary: we must decide what the Senate's role and functions are in the new architecture and deduce from this the most coherent system of election. It is clear, then, that a Senate that is the seat of representation of the territorial legislative institutions cannot but be connected to the regional councils (and I do not even believe that it can deprive itself of the Presidents of the Regions so as not to give them the opportunity to play on two tables). But, in the same way, a Senate that contributes to primary constitutional functions cannot be removed from the sovereignty of the electoral body. In this sense, the idea of selecting senators during the election of the Regional Councils was there from the beginning of the reform process, carved in our amendments not as downward mediation but as an original proposal able to reconcile the different peculiarities of the future Senate. So it could have come a long way before, saving on the path of reforms tensions and torsions that certainly did not benefit. On this and on other aspects the text can and must be improved. Not in the sense of giving the Senate an improper role of counterweight, because counterweights are to be sought elsewhere in a system logic, but by restoring functions that in the various readings have been lost in the street, guaranteeing it an effective weight in the election of constitutional judges, involving it in the connection with Europe, in the control and evaluation of public policies. Again, I proposed an amendment to sculpt in the Constitution the equal dignity between the different areas of our country, between North and South. Not only do we say yes to reforms, but until the last moment we will work to give the country the best possible reforms. The awareness of the moment we are living requires us, however, Mr President, not to stop at the reading of this text. The institutions are in fact like an organism, that lives in space and in political time and that demands balance, proportion and an intrinsic harmony between the different parts that compose it. In order to seek this balance, the Letta Government proposed a reinforced delegation to Article 138, so that the two branches of Parliament could deal in an organic design with the form of State, the form of Government, bicameralism and electoral law. I am not here to retrace the vicissitudes that blocked this attempt at the moment of its arrival in port, but it is a fact that today we are to discuss a new system that serves the fragmentation of its construction and that, precisely for this reason, requires the willingness to proceed for successive adjustments in a politically useful time. Furthermore, Madam President, the decision to postpone the entry into force of the new electoral law to next year is also a response to the need for an area for the search for a system balance. If not, why is it that choice, if not to have an area of harmonization? What does the literal law have to do with the reform on our examination? This has something to do with it, Madam President. It is because the combination between Italicum and Senate reform will introduce a new political system in our country, and with the same clarity with which we support the reforms we cannot but pose the problem of the face that Italy will have a minute later and of which political cultures will have the right to citizenship in Italy. I call things by their name and say that a system that banishes coalitions today risks banning liberal, Catholic, secular culture moderated by political representation. The new rules, grafted on the current panorama, can block the democratic game in a clash between system and anti-system in which the forces of the system remain the only alternative between the dissolution in social democracy or disappear. And it is clear that this prospect poses a problem of no small moment for those of us who have guaranteed the path of reform not because they had changed their minds about their place, but because they believed that there was a need to write new common rules without this meaning to confuse or cancel their identity. You see, ladies and gentlemen, for a long time the Italian Left has been crossed by a debate between those who advocated membership of the European Social Democratic Family and those who, instead, like Pietro Scoppola, thought that the current PD should enclose a plurality of traditions and cultures, including the Catholic democratic one. The issue has been resolved in this legislature, when a post-ideological leader has ferryed the Democratic Party into the European Socialist Party. We have witnessed with great respect this identity choice, but it must involve an awareness of the importance of cultural roots and not the claim to deprive other cultures and other political families of the right to citizenship. In short, just now that the PD has dissolved all ambiguity and embraced social democracy, it would be paradoxical that the Italian popular forces would lose by law every space of autonomy and right of representation. This is not a hyperbola: the combination of reforms and Italicum with a prize to the list would replicate, amplifying it as a result of the ballot, the phenomenon of the last Europeans, when the fear of a victory of anti-politics conveyed transversal consensus towards the social-democratic proposal assigning it the forced monopoly of common sense. We have been told that the list premium gives greater guarantees to the stability of governments. It is true, but precisely the parable of the PdL, of which we have been protagonists, shows that anti-fragmentation aggregations do not in themselves guarantee the duration of the executives in the absence of stabilisation mechanisms that refer to that now mythical agenda Perassi, still waiting for implementation. We are also told: You could have thought of that before. You also voted for Italicum. I respond to this with an objection and with a dispute. The argument is that we've thought about it. I have collected a wide anthology of parliamentary interventions, interviews, statements, which testify that the subject of the amendment of the electoral law with the prediction of the coalition prize was placed in due time and in every place. And certainly he would have been raised to the House if there had not been a confidence on purpose that I have always considered inappropriate and whose political impact today is fully understood. The first is Tatarella, who died in 1999, of whom I consider myself a political disciple (and I am proud of it), who in 1995 invented a law, which was recalled in these hours, to solve the problem: election or designation. In 1995 there had been a backlash, Parliament was in a state of uncertainty, there was a centre-right majority, the Dini Government and a confused situation; regional elections were looming and the direct election of the Presidents of the Regions was being made. To this end, the Constitution would have to be changed, but there was no time: it was December-January and it would be voted in April. Then Tatarella said that it was necessary to make a law that guaranteed a popular nomination-election of the President of the Region, a bipolar scheme and a majority. Tatarella then dictated the principles of a law that has been applied for a long time (in some regions, although with some modifications, the plant has sometimes remained), which provided for a list on the condition that it had won to obtain a majority prize, and thus led to a bipolarism. In addition, this law provided for the coalition to indicate a candidate, such as "Mario Rossi," as president of the Region. However, as the election of the people could not take place because there was no time to change the Constitution, this designation became somewhat politically binding on the Regional Council. So much so that the citizens went to the vote and the nominated candidates were elected as presidents of the Regions, However, not being that election strengthened by the Constitution, happened the rivetons (I remember Rastrelli, president of the Campania Region, and Nisticò, president of the Calabria Region) in the course of work because of second thoughts; there was some friends of Verdini in circulation also at the time. Then the majorities changed and other presidents took over. Then the constitutional reform was carried out, today in force with the popular election of the presidents of the Region. Tatarella made a law that allowed for an increase in direct democracy, since there was no time to consolidate it with the reform of the Constitution, which was done later. It would take two steps and the rites we know. I repeat, it was January and we would have voted in April 2005, so there was no time. I believe that the government has rediscovered the possibility of an indication of these future, supposed senators, who will not be elected by the citizens; there will be some sort of designation, then the Regional Council will choose. "Behind every article in this Constitution, young people, you must see young people like you, fallen fighting, shot, hanged, tortured, starving in concentration camps, dead in Russia, Africa, dead in the streets of Milan, on the streets of Florence, who gave their lives so that freedom and justice could be written on this Charter. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u69	Madam President, Madam Representative of the Government, fellow Senators, we are called upon to analyse the constitutional reform measure. But what is a reform? The Treccani vocabulary defines it as a process of modification of something directed to its improvement. Well, this definition is opposed to this bill, which was born with the ambitious aim of adapting our Constitution, not only to the needs of today's society, but, above all, to ensure, to our young people and future generations, an efficient state based on solid democratic foundations. Alas, the result is a constitutional reproach. To determine this decline was the obtuse and obstinate will of the Government to continue in a solitary race, without confrontation, nor sharing, humiliating the Senate in a general sense and, in particular, its Commission, first replacing the representatives of majority "not aligned" to the thought of course, then interrupting the legislative process and, finally, deciding the changes to make to the text exclusively within the direction of the Democratic Party, rather than looking in this House - the natural place where it was to be done - for a wider sharing. At the beginning of this reform path, we at Forza Italia chose to offer full collaboration to the majority, respecting the constituent spirit, having as its sole objective the good of the country, convinced that the change of rules should take place exclusively through the participation of all responsible forces. This was the case and, just a year ago, the Senate fired an acceptable text, which I decided to vote even though it did not coincide with my ideal of constitutional installation, but, however, it was worthy of support. The transition to the House, on the other hand, has upset the nature of the reform, making it unacceptable and, I would say, even dangerous for at least two reasons: the functions and the composition of the new Senate. The amendments to Articles 1 and 10, in particular, have drastically reduced the powers initially conferred on the Senate, giving it in fact a role so secondary that it makes it totally useless, we have to tell us. Then I wonder why we have to keep an empty box out of function. It is absolutely better to abolish it and move directly to a single-cameral system, to which in fact this reform wants to bring us. In addition, a Chamber composed of senators no longer elected by the people, but appointed by the territorial institutions, makes this House a mere after-work, a cemetery of elephants, certainly necessary to the majority chair to park its professional politicians at the end of their career. It is incomprehensible that the judgment of the sovereign people, expressed through direct elections, replaces the superpower of the Secretary of the Democratic Party. I am convinced that precisely that sovereign people, when they are called to speak on the necessary confirmation referendum, will firmly punish this reform. Moreover, the changes to the functions and compositions of the new Senate, in conjunction with the new electoral law, Italicum, will determine the failure of that check and balance between the different powers of the State that represents the hinge, the heart and the democratic soul on which our Constitution is founded. The new rules, in fact, allow those who win the elections to obtain three hundred and forty Members of the House, useful to exercise an uncontested power that gives into the hands - let me say it explicitly - of the secretary of the majority party, whoever it is, all the highest positions of the State: the President of the Republic, the President of the Chamber, the President of the Council, the Government, the Authorities, the control of the Higher Council of the judiciary and, directly or indirectly, the majority of the Constitutional Court (ten out of fifteen). Therefore, with full conviction, I can neither share nor vote for a new Constitution which, rather than broadening democratic guarantees, represents an authoritarian drift of the State in which there will be no effective control and limitation to the overpower of a single political component. I would like to close with a textual quote: The way in which this Government and this majority proceed (...) was the opposite of what was followed in this Chamber, in the constituent Assembly, by the Government, by the majority and by the opposition of that time (...). You have moved forward, with this foolish reform, contrary to the example of the Constituent, only so as not to bring down the Government (...). Once again, on this occasion, emerges the conception that belongs to this Government and this majority, according to which the winner of the elections owns the institutions, is its owner. This is a mistake. It is a profoundly wrong conception. The institutions are of everyone, of those who are in government and of those who are in opposition. The serious thing is that, this time, the victim of your conception is our Constitution. I address you, President Mattarella, to treasure your thoughts ten years ago and to prevent Italy from turning into something so dangerous that I do not even want to mention. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u70	Madam President, Madam Representative of the Government, I will make a few comments to justify once again the agreement of the People's Area Group to this constitutional reform, which is no secondary reason for the life of this legislature, to which we have contributed significantly. First of all, I would like to point out that overcoming the perfect bicameralism is at last the breakup of that constitutional compromise imposed by the Communist Party, which has for a long time given rise to a slow and often blocked democracy. People like Pacciardi, first, Craxi, then, and Berlusconi himself were accused of authoritarianism for having hypothesized a ruling democracy that today, albeit on the basis of inevitable compromises, can reasonably be achieved. But I want to focus even more on that part of the constitutional reform that concerns Title V of Part II and therefore the righting of the crooked tree of Italian federalism. We have supported you with specific amendments, with the intention, first of all, of rebuilding the unitary state, convinced as we are that a nation - and we are a nation - must correspond to a state. On the contrary, the nation came before the state and forged itself on the common principles of Christian tradition; then, laboriously, on it a weak unitary state was realized, which we have further weakened over the last decades through a disordered and irresponsible federalism. We have helped to shift competences - as already proposed in the Berlusconi reform - from the regions to the state (I am referring to energy, strategic infrastructure, large networks, but also to work and the environment); I hope that no amendment will be amended. In the same way, we shared the possibility of creating a federalism with variable geometry, indicating the functions - and we are available to a possible strengthening of them - that could be required by virtuous regions, in the condition, that is, to be able to carry them out properly. But above all we have worked for the clause of state supremacy referred to in Article 31 of the draft law, that which determines the new article 117, fourth paragraph, of the Constitution, through which the State, beyond the distribution of competences, can intervene on everything, when it warns that the protection of the legal or economic unity of the Republic is in question, or the protection of the national interest: first the State. As a result, we have tabled amendments which, albeit with reformulation, have been accepted and which have concerned the assumption in the constitutional dictation of the so-called standard costs and requirements, referred to in the law for fiscal federalism, which have been codified in article 33 of the reform, including them in article 119, fourth paragraph, of the Constitution; standard costs and requirements that are the instruments of limited sovereignty, limited autonomy of the Regions and the Municipalities; limited and responsible, because responsible. With our amendment to the original Article 33 (now Article 34), accepted with reformulation by the Government and then voted by this very House, we wanted to introduce the Commissariat not only of the Municipalities, but also of the Regions. Who, like me, has had the albeit brief experience of Minister of Health and has considered it necessary to commissioner a large part of the Regions of the Centre-South, given their serious condition of financial failure, regarding the fundamental voice of the regional health service, can testify to the difficulty of restoring to balance those conditions of failure according to an obligatory negotiation with the Presidents of the Regions; and so, the commissioners of the regional health have necessarily been identified in the Presidents of the Region, discussing with them arduously one or more subcommissisari concretely dedicated to the plans of return, in relation to the limits of the present constitutional structure (standard costs and needs, commissioners, instruments of a responsible autonomy, instruments of that spending review that in the next period we will have to realize not only for the State, but also for those two thirds of the current expenditure that are represented by the Municipalities and the Regions). Today, the Ministry of Economy, through the Sose, the company in charge of the surveys functional to the costs and the requirements standard, can even produce the algorithm according to which to generate a timely commissioning of the municipal functions, that is, when it produces the imbalance that the standard requirements allow to detect; that structural imbalance that today instead deepens until a condition of failure that, when detected, makes the situation really difficult reversible, as testimonies great Municipalities and great municipalities like Naples or Rome. We adhere therefore with conviction to this reform, not only for the part that has been mainly the subject of public confrontation, but also in some ways especially for the part that reconstructs the unity of the Nation, through the prevalence of the national interest and the return to responsibility of the Municipalities and Regions [[Applause from Senator Torrisi.]]
