ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u80	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, with trepidation I crossed the threshold of this House about fifteen days ago and with a bit of emotion I feel in this first speech which is of fundamental importance with regard to our Constitution. For the role we play we have a huge job to do with responsibility and conscience, because in my opinion we depend on the survival of people, their feelings, their daily life and the future of our nation. Without making behindology that usually smokes intrigue of the palace, capturing the sterile controversy, it is in the eyes of all the unshakable will of the Government and a substantial part of the PD to continue in a solitary race, without confrontation or sharing, thus engaging in acrobatic palace games or political secretariats, while the country - alas - is sinking in total political, civil and institutional indifference. With these behaviors you give an image, in my view, unupbuilding. We quarrel over everything, and these constitutional changes, rather than reflecting a long, in-depth and constructive debate, as our constituent Fathers did, are marked by permanent clashes between parties and especially within the majority party, while, in the meantime, small and medium-sized enterprises fail or close, increase the unemployed, increase the army of young people or over fifty without work or those distrusted who refuse even to seek it. This government, which would have the objective of transmitting a positive charge of youthful enthusiasm and futuristic dynamism, cannot then approve a hybrid bill on the prerogatives of the Senate, not caring for the errors and contradictions present in this reform that concern nature, composition and function. I want - if you will allow me - to emphasize three. The first contradiction concerns the functions of the Senate: if in a first phase the right balance had been found between the functions between the Chamber and the Senate and, with the autonomy necessary to exercise the function of control, it had been able to transpose the instances of the Regions and the Municipalities, because of the changes this balance has been distorted, effectively canceling the prerogatives of the Senate and the Regions, eliminating any function of connection and control. The Senate remains only a function of participation in those which were of its own direct competence, thus turning out to be a mere appendix to the Chamber of Deputies. The second concerns the electivity of the Senate: electing it indirectly, there would be no possibility of choice or even of knowledge on the part of the elector. I am also referring to the way voters choose for the metropolitan cities, and I seriously start to think that the will to expel the people from the possibility of choosing their representatives directly is now a fisima of this government. The result of the law abolishing the Provinces, then, is in the eyes of everyone: there has only been a multiplication of costs and armchairs, thus giving rise to a harmful reform that deceives Europe and the Italians, with the result of raising the costs of politics and canceling the participation of the citizens. As for competing subjects, the Senate, considering its functions in the new version, turns out to be a real recreational circle with its unnecessary costs. Articles 1 and 10 of the draft law under consideration, in particular, have undergone major changes in the transition to the Chamber of Deputies, all in the sense of a drastic reduction in the functions, both legislative and non-legislative, attributed to the Senate at first reading. They are no longer autonomous and/or exclusive competence, in fact, all those functions of evaluation, verification and control, proper to a Chamber released from the trust relationship with the Government, thus condemning the High Chamber to a constant subalternity with respect to the decisions of the Chamber of Deputies. The text adopted at first reading, albeit with some shortcomings, was - in my view - a balanced reform. In the combined arrangement of this reform, however, only one party is allowed to exercise an uncontested power, not only within the Chamber of Deputies, but in the relations between the House and the Senate, in those between the State and the Regions, in the election of the President of the Republic and, finally, in the composition of the Constitutional Court. I could go on for a long time, but I think it is more important to put another aspect to the attention of this sum, or rather - forgive me - a question: are we really convinced that we can solve the problems of the people who suffer, who do not have a job, who cannot eat, starting from a constitutional reform, fighting for the same for months and paralyzing what structural reforms should be, such as tax reductions, incentives, incentives to companies, battlehorses of this Government? I urge you, therefore, to make every useful effort to find a shared path on important issues, overcoming divisions. In this dramatic situation, we need a common path, using, above all, a common language. This provision has been discussed for a long time, but reasoning does not mean succumbing to the logic of numbers. Today politics is perceived as cryptic, astute, self-referential, cynical and cold. Let us try to include in the vocabulary of politics terms such as participation, involvement, comparison. If we succeed in finding a shared path on these important issues, we will say that we were the generation that, in a spirit of responsibility, was able to indicate a new path, because animated by the spirit of common belonging to a nation that found the strength to react in a moment of great difficulty. If not, I will see, in spite of this, that we are faced with a wall, at a time when in Europe and in the world they are trying to destroy any kind of wall, because they have understood that we are united by a common destiny, that it will be of trust and hope, only if we can meet on the things that unite, putting aside those that divide us. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u81	Senator Torrisi is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u82	Madam President, representative of the Government, fellow senators, the Italian Constitution undoubtedly reflects the fundamental and inalienable principles that, if we do not want to lose, we must integrate with those innovations that will allow Italy to be competitive in Europe and in the world. If other countries react rapidly to change and globalisation, it is certainly due to systems that are more projected towards growth and modernisation rather than conservation. Not reforming our institutional architecture - it doesn't take long to understand it - is a luxury that, today, we can no longer afford: we need to simplify and speed up, but also cut the costs of politics and the times of decisions. The country has been waiting for this reform for too long, there have been many unsuccessful attempts and promises. The debate in this House, at the third reading, serves to remind us that the political, parliamentary and cultural debate on overcoming the bicameralism of equality, for which the Chamber of Deputies and the Senate do the same things and have the same powers, has always found in agreement most scholars and politicians and also in the present Parliament and in the current party structure is absolutely prevalent. Few in Italy question the political unity of the country to direct the prevailing activity of a second Chamber; once stripped of the power to express confidence to the Government, it must be a Chamber of Regions and territorial autonomy. Given that the issue of bicameralism can be resolved because it is widely shared, the political and parliamentary confrontation must concern the reconstruction of relations between the organs of the State, so as to define the new constitutional and institutional balances. This is the path that must be taken into consideration, that is to better identify the functions of the new Senate in order to recover elements of guarantee and balance. We must recover a political and constitutional culture that strengthens and defines the specific reason for the new Senate. Overcoming double trust for governments and a drastic cut in the number of senators is no small thing. They are fair flags for collecting consensus in the country and in the European Union. Under them we must build a project that must have strength and credibility and, therefore, the parliamentary confrontation must focus on how to properly organize the transformation of the Senate into a democratic institution in which the representatives of the territories can express themselves and contribute to the great mission of the unification of the country. The specific reason for the new Senate is the exercise of functions of liaison between the State and the other constituent bodies of the Republic and between them and the European Union. But what does this connection consist of and how does it work? What representative qualities do the new senators need to have to perform it? What is the constitutional balance resulting from the introduction of such a connecting house? To the first question modern constitutionalism answers that the only way to connect the institutions of a complex society is to network with them; it is illusory, as well as constitutionally illegitimate, to suppress and absorb them. Networking is also the only possible defense in the face of the annomic forces that, outside the state and law, tend to condition the world and life. It is not enough to write a link as a label on an empty box; the constitutional rules also need to establish mechanisms and cooperation procedures for this link to be implemented. The Constitutional Court and many lawyers have long been calling for this: a constitutional framework for suitable instruments to ensure cooperation between the different levels of government and defuse the conflict between them. We need a political body, a Chamber of Independents, but equipped with the tools to network within the State, and outside, in the European Union, because in the supranational order the second Chambers have the same weight as the first and every European norm must inexorably confront on the ground of autonomy with subsidiarity and proportionality. On this level, the text needs to be improved. In fact, Enzo Cheli argues that: In a constitutional reform text, all parties are strongly interconnected as expressions of a complex and coherent system where everything is held. It is a well-founded thesis, reinforced by the intention to convene a referendum on an entire project confirming its close unity. A rationalization is therefore still possible in order to bring the good of the project to fruition; good not only for our state, but also for the European Union, where political stability is interdependent like the economy. By collecting also the contributions that come from constitutionalists animated by this spirit and that share these assumptions, acquired the fact that it is important to approve the reform in this legislature and that the members of the Senate will be the representatives of territorial and local autonomy, we concentrate the parliamentary commitment on the functions to be attributed to this institution, which have been reduced in the transition to the House, to organize them to the best in order to carry out the mission that we intend to give them. Unfortunately, most of the controversy and criticism is directed at how to prevent the reform from being completed. Perhaps he was wrong in considering the two cornerstones of the reform (elimination of joint bicameralism and Senate as representative of the autonomy) and not to increase the discussion on the political-cultural justification of its need and on the concrete problems of its implementation. But it is still possible to achieve this objective with improved amendments and a calm debate that can improve the quality of the constitutional text. The other important issue that has strongly divided the political and parliamentary confrontation concerns the composition of the new Senate, on which it is possible to find a high mediation strengthening the choice to build the second Chamber as an assembly that really brings the voice of regional and local institutions to the center of the State, in an authoritative way, maximum expression of the principle of autonomy affirmed as fundamental in the Constitution. There are different paths, which have already been proposed by members of the People's Area, such as Senator Quagliariello, and by the same president of the 1st Finocchiaro Commission, who could overcome the antithesis between a Senate of nominees and one with direct and universal election. The conflict on this point of the reform is likely to frustrate the reform and once again to delegitimize the whole of Parliament because of its inability to sum up. The spread seemed irreconcilable, two armed camps engaged in a sort of religious war. And like all wars of religion, this blinds and does not allow to see simple and practicable solutions. I believe there is a political and technical space, which is emerging, to find the meeting between the different positions. The political theme of a constitutional reform that has been discussed for decades is not so much direct or indirect election, but if Italy is ready to overcome the perfect bicameralism in favour of governance. This is indeed the crucial point of a new institutional and parliamentary structure, which leaves senators a role of direction and control, but deprives them of the power to give confidence to the government, overcomes the perfect bicameralism and greatly reduces them in number. Perhaps it would be more fair and more straightforward for Italian citizens to say whether or not they believe that this reform can make Italy a more modern country or if they are afraid, instead, that the doors will open to the risk of a reduction of democracy. A single Chamber that makes laws and supports the Government or distrust undoubtedly simplifies the system, makes majorities more stable, speeds legislative action, makes clearer merits and responsibilities, lights the spotlight on the behaviour of individual parliamentarians without the need to introduce mandate constraints, as Rescigno argues. This does not weaken our democracy. As far as the reform of bicameralism is concerned, I would like to stress that the choice of equalism by the constituents, for the political reasons of that historical moment, represented a deviation from the models of the form of parliamentary government. In fact, the form of parliamentary government is based on the trust relationship not with every House of Parliament, but only with that which directly expresses popular sovereignty. The form of parliamentary government can only be strengthened, and not weakened, by overcoming the anomalous Italian bicameralism. There is certainly a need for limits to government power, but these limits lie in the President of the Republic, the Constitutional Court and the CSM elected with quorums that escape the majority; they are in the investigative judiciary completely autonomous from the Government, they are in the vertical separation of powers, in referendums and above all in the role of opposition, which is fundamental in the parliamentary systems (where Executive and Legislative are merged by trust and not separated as in pure presidential systems) and which therefore needs to be strengthened. Whoever loses must have real powers of control over the work of Government, but who loses is in the Political Chamber, and not in that representative of the territorial institutions. Action can still be taken to improve relations between state bodies. In fact, every power of the national State has its own field of competence and the prerogatives that that competence allows it to exercise. This correct vision has as its historical source the rule of law theorized by Montesquieu. This is the premise of every democracy and we must intervene - as Stefano Rodotà reminds us - in order to ensure greater participation of that new organization of social powers which goes under the name of counter-democracy. In this sense it is not a crime, both for those who want this reform of the Senate, and for those who do not want it, to question the new electoral law, which offers the majority prize to the party and not to the coalition that takes more votes. The electoral law and Senate reform are directly involved in this view of the rule of law, because - as Passigli believes - it must be stressed that all democratic systems are based on an essential common denominator of balance between powers. For these reasons, the People's Area believes that further action must be taken, certainly after the approval of the constitutional reform, and after an important cultural and institutional reflection on the electoral law, providing for the modification of the coalition award to overcome the choice, which today appears hyper-major, made with the so-called Italicum for the election of the House. Italicum is an electoral system that values the theme of governance, but penalizes and mortifies the issue of representation, which is equally important, allowing even a party with just 30% to win the elections and obtain 55% of the polling stations. With a coalition system, if well used by political forces, the important objective can be achieved in democracies to better represent the majority of citizens and to have greater electoral legitimacy. Let bicameralism be eliminated, but keep that supreme good which is the balance between powers. An overall view of all institutional reforms - constitutional and electoral reform - and also the discipline of political parties are necessary to verify and develop the overall system of balances and so-called weights and counterweights. Of course, we must intervene by having clearly and knowing how to respect the sense of the limit, as President Napolitano has observed, because we cannot start from scratch, do and undo the canvas at this stage of constitutional reform, determining for the umpteenth time an unforgivable failure and, also, determining a political crisis that would prevent us to face the other issues that are dear to the Italians (the cutting of taxes, employment, the modernization of the State, the fight against corruption and the end of public waste). We create the political and constituent climate that will allow us to proceed to a balanced reform without unnecessary forcing, indeed with the corrections necessary to approve it with a wider and more solid majority... ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u100	Senator Amidei is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u101	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, I rise to reflect on this constitutional reform. We have spoken in many, more or less sharing not so much the content, but the method. It is above all on the method, but also on the content, that I wish to speak, making some reflections. The decision of Forza Italia to collaborate in the process of constitutional reform was of seriousness and made for the good of the country and in the certainty that changing the rules must be a common path and to be done together with all responsible forces. In addition, the underlying system contained some - albeit not all - elements that have been the historical heritage of Forza Italia: the end of the perfect bicameralism and the subjects of concurrent competence between the State and the Regions, which in recent years have caused immense damage in the management of the public thing. This sharing process was interrupted by the Democratic Party's decision to unilaterally elect President Mattarella in an attempt to recompose internal fractures. The transition to the Chamber of Deputies, the changes made and the obtuse and unshakable will of the Government and the Democratic Party to continue in a solitary race without confrontation, nor sharing have radically changed the situation. Our judgment on this reform, therefore, can only be negative. As we have seen, the central points concerning Senate reform are the following: Article 1 amending Article 55 of the Constitution concerning the nature of the Senate; Article 2 amending Article 57 of the Constitution on the composition of the Senate; Article 10 amending Article 70 of the Constitution on legislative functions. Articles 1 and 10, in particular, have undergone major changes in the transition to the Chamber of Deputies, which have all gone in the direction of a drastic reduction in the legislative and non-legislative functions attributed to the Senate at first reading. They are no longer autonomous or exclusive competence, in fact, all functions in the matter of evaluation, verification and control of public policies, of connection between the State and the Regions, of financial autonomy of the local authorities and of legislative autonomy of the Regions own of a Chamber released from the trust relationship with the Government, thus condemning the Upper House to a constant subordinateness with respect to the decisions of the other Chamber. The Senate, which in this new version is quite secondary, is therefore reduced to that after-work of the regional councillors that someone had already predicted. This real annulment of the Senate is due to the weakness of the source of legitimacy deriving from the method of election of senators provided for in Article 2 of the provision. That is why we are calling for a return to a direct election of senators that enhances the popular will and guarantees the recognition of candidates by the elector, in order to give back to the Senate the role necessary to draw a balanced constitutional structure. The text adopted at first reading was, albeit with some shortcomings, a balanced reform that was very attentive to the checks and balances of the different levels of state. With the combined arrangement of this reform and Italicum, only one party is allowed, which requires only 20-25 percent of the votes to gain a majority prize, to exercise an uncontested power, not only within the Chamber, but also in the relations between the House and the Senate and between the State and the Regions, as well as in the election of the President of the Republic and in the composition of the Constitutional Court. Another amendment to the Chamber of Deputies concerns precisely the way in which the judges of the Constitutional Court are elected. The text approved in the Senate provided that of the five elected judges, three were elected by the Chamber and two by the Senate, while the subsequent amendment refers to the modalities currently provided for in the Constitution, i.e. the election of the five judges by the Parliament in common session. However, it should be borne in mind that the numerical relationship between the two rooms has been profoundly altered. We are talking about six hundred and thirty Members against one hundred senators and, therefore, the weight of the senators in the choice of judges of the Constitutional Court becomes virtually zero. Article 31 of the provision amends Article 117 of the Constitution, concerning the division of legislative powers between the State and the Regions. An article that should have sanctioned the sacrosanct principle of clarity of legislative powers and competences, if true clarity had been made. Unfortunately, it doesn't seem that way. The reading of the new Article 117, in fact, gives rise to serious doubts, already from the multiplicity and variety of terms used to indicate legislative competences, fundamental principles, coordination rules, general principles, general order profiles. They all seem generic terms and facts placed to seek and create confusion. Moreover, if we look at the relationship between the State and the Regions that derives from the arranged combination of a new Senate called to represent the territorial institutions and a new division of legislative competences between the State and the Regions, there is the doubt that the archaic concurrent competence still exists, even under disguised remains. It recites the dossier prepared by the Study Office of this Chamber that the abolition of the concurrent legislative competence does not often result in mere bipartition in exclusive state or regional competence, but rather a irradiation or eviction in multiple types of competence cohabitations between State and Regions. From various parts, including the majority of the Regions, complains of the result of having a regional council of monco part of its members called to compose a Senate representative of the local institutions without however there being any bond with the institution of origin. The question of the Provinces must also be addressed, albeit briefly. The Delrio law had begun to empty its powers in view of their abolition, which in this reform actually takes place, apparently. In fact, the present reform constitutionalizes the existence of large-area entities, administrative bodies that without any popular legitimacy will partly inherit the responsibilities of the Provinces. Mr President, fellow senators, dear fellow citizens, if the vote in the coming days is what the government and the majority expect, the history of the Senate as a legislative chamber will be finally concluded. Is that good or bad? The question is not rhetorical and the answer is not obvious. It is good or bad, as in all things, according to what will prevail. Trying to synthesize in a few minutes questions on which constitutionalists poured rivers of ink, there is no doubt that perfect bicameralism was an anomaly only Italian. An anomaly born with precise historical reasons, which for a long time was considered no longer to have any reason to be, but which today, by virtue of the different electoral systems of the Chamber and Senate, has returned to have a meaning, problematic from the point of view of governance, but strong from the point of view of control and guarantees. This is a value that we will certainly lose. The combined arrangement of the new electoral law and the abolition of the powers of the Senate means that a political force capable of obtaining 25% of the votes could govern our country without counterweights and mediations. Is that good or bad? I leave the answer to your reflection. A reflection that must also take into account the extraordinary fall in voters, with 25% of the valid votes corresponding to 12-13% of voters. It is a strange democracy, the one in which 13% of voters choose the government, which will be able to operate for five years without counterweights. The democratic conscience of our fellow Members should take a thorough look at this before we vote on our own decision. Of course, perfect bicameralism was no longer sustainable. It is a system that does not apply anywhere in the world. But it is true that every country has its history, its characteristics, its traditions: otherwise the Constitutions could all be the same. But there are only two logical ways to overcome the perfect bicameralism. One is very simple: it is enough to abolish the Senate. Unmistakably abolishing it. Monocameral systems are no less democratic than bicameral systems. The United Kingdom has for a long time been a substantially monocameral system, the House of Lords performs almost only honorary and symbolic functions, yet it is the oldest and most solid democracy in the world. Monocameralism is a choice, questionable, but that has a systemic consistency. The other way is to differentiate the competences between the Senate and the Chamber, giving the Senate the role of a specialized legislative assembly, perhaps detached from trust to the Government, with strong powers of control and rebalancing. In this case, it is difficult to find a logical reason why it should not be elected by the citizens. Why take away from voters the right to choose who represents them? Why assign it to the Regional Councils? To save money on ballots and electoral pencils? It's a little weak in democracy. Yet the non-electivity of the Senate seems to be one of the insurmountable limits from which the Government and the majority do not intend to ignore for any reason. I'm going back to asking, why? Why entrust to a local political class, which has, among other things, given bad self-esteem in recent times, a power that could be much more legitimately exercised by citizens? The fact is that this reform contains a flaw at the root. The fault of not choosing. He doesn't choose to abolish the Senate, but he doesn't even choose to give him tasks. He chooses only to make it survive, as an institutional larva, not elected, without a clear role, composed of regional advisors who will live it as a post-worker, or as the place where to make goods of power, influence, territorial lobbying or corporate. Precisely the phenomena that are the basis - much more than bicameralism - of the slowness and contradiction of Italian legislative production. What's the new senate gonna do? It will have a regional council of part of its members, called to compose a Senate representative of the local institutions without however there being any bond with the institution of origin. In the end, then, Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, dear fellow citizens, what can be said about this reform of the Constitution? Does it reform, transform, or deform? And what's deformed? The real nature? Yes, so the true nature of the inspiring principles of the Constitution is distorted, creating an unformed Senate, which resembles a small monster with uncertain and indefinable connotations, or a small Chamber, or perhaps a closet. At this point, we might as well have eliminated the Senate altogether. Thank you. We're worried. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII. Congratulations.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u104	Senator Romani Maurizio is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u105	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to start from the concept that the Republican Constitution, like all the Constitutions, was in essence a policy aimed at establishing some fundamental rules of collective life, that the initial conditions wanted open in its elaboration to the contribution of all political forces and destined, like all Constitutions, to rebuild an order that had been disturbed for various reasons. It was precisely an opening to the most diverse contributions that was contained in the option of convening a constituent assembly elected by universal suffrage with a proportional system and of delivering an elaborate one, together with the judgment of political forces and their representatives, avoiding referendum confirmations that could have denied it, either by calling into question other actors (at that time the Church or the survivors of the past regime) or by producing different political alignments. If there was little of a shared political culture, basically it needed very little; it needed the ability to say and not to say, to promise without too much commitment to maintain; as Piero Calamandrei will say years later, it was above all necessary to calm the waters. Given that for the political cultures of the time everything was favorable to the parties, but they were not unbridled supporters of their pluralism, if there were those who looked towards Moscow, there was not even a lack of those who looked towards Lisbon or Madrid. As for the often considered more innovative part of the Constitution, that concerning the protection of the job, the restrictions of the property rights, the social rights and the intervention of the State in the economy, it found impulse in the prevailing presence among the benches of the Assembly constituent representatives of socialist, communist and Catholic-popular extraction, but it is difficult to define it as much more than a set of promises. The views of the three mass parties were also very different and even within them there were no lack of differences. Aware of this multipolarism, of the dramatic tensions underlying it and of the risks involved, the different poles decided to regulate it, adopting democratic-representative institutions. So it is useless to look in the Italian Constitution for proof of a shared culture. It is a document written in very difficult times, by a multiplicity of very different political actors, who with it testified the will to close one page of national history and to open another. Throughout history, the Constitution has been widely used for everything it represented and for what it was written in its articles. And what it originally represented was a multipolar configuration of society and national politics. In the last three decades, in Italy and throughout the West, the idea of a multipolar society has been replaced by that of a unipolar society, in which everyone would agree on the foundations of associated life. Among the shortcomings of which Italy seems to be afflicted, there is that of a robust shared political culture; a well-functioning democracy cannot help it. In reality, that of shared culture is a prejudice that always circulates in democratic thinking, forgetting just how from the conflict modern democratic regimes were born and that, if the conflict can be disruptive, where democratically regulated is a source of vitality for democracy itself. The problem is that humans are very different from each other, that only occasionally in the real world they think the same way, that erase or reduce diversity is an impossible task. We can pretend to achieve it, but it is not at all said, as it is not said that an excess of consensus really benefits democracy, given that it, in addition to being a child of difference, pluralism and conflict, lives of difference, pluralism and conflict. For this reason, the convergence of constituents to no one appears to be the result of a common political culture. This writing took place in a situation of bitter social, cultural and political conflict, and could not have taken place otherwise. Then we will be able to attribute to the constituents the intention to sign together a document that would refound the coexistence between the Italians. But, in the same way, I can also assume that the real orientation was to divide the conduct of the country; or equally, I can believe that the Constitution was used to celebrate the victory against fascism and, finally, that it was only a dilatory move, filled with winks, hypocrisies and mutual concessions. But there is no doubt that for the political class the problem was to dismiss the past as soon as possible, to introduce institutional constraints that would prevent its reappearance, to legitimize, even in the eyes of the international public opinion, the new regime, as well as to recognize the powers that had emerged in the meantime, that is to say the political parties, preventing the prevalence of other powers of fact looming and more difficult to metabolize democratically (the allies on the one hand, the Vatican on the other). (15.15) (Romani Maurizio followed). Therefore, democratic rules had to, at least in the immediate future, promote coexistence between parties, reducing the risks of mutual overwhelmment and laying down the conditions for a government action that alleviated the social problems that many considered amplified and aggravated by fascism and conflict. The parties had become, in the aftermath of the fall of fascism, new institutions, protagonists of public life. Let us remember that fascism had been against the parties and therefore the rehabilitation of the parties and their pluralism was, at least at that time, a duty to pay tribute to restored democracy, overcoming also the liberal tradition in which politics had to be privileged reserve of the notables, the class owners and the educated. This tradition will remain as vital as ever in post-war Italy, until it reaches us, often contaminating itself with right-wing extremism and nourishing an anti-party and anti-political sentiment worthy heir of 19th-century anti-parliamentaryism. We have thus come to the degradation of the country's political culture, which in the 1980s suffered a rapid process of individualistic, particularistic and territorial disintegration; hence the disordered and ephemeral success of the moralizing demands of civil society and the electoral rooting of a secessionist party that has relocated to the extreme right a part of the electorate and moderate classes. I have said that democracy is a mediation technique, but it is also a child of conflict. In Italy the great conflict - that of class - seems to have extinguished or dispersed in thousand rivoli. What Cesare Pinelli defined as the dilemma between constitutional democracy and populist democracy, which contrasts an idea of hypopolitical democracy with a hyperpoliticalized, but both veined of anti-politics, has assumed tones that it is difficult not to consider worrying. Failed the attempts to renew from within the old political class and the traditional parties, the craving for change and the anti-political sign that had been imprinted favored the "descent in the field" of new actors who, together with other hastily converted to democracy, after being for a long time the most severe critics, have hegemonized the moderate and conservative components of society and politics. There are no more visions of the world alternative to neoliberal orthodoxy, and this has brought political programmes considerably closer together. Times change and political culture is now manufactured through the media and in front of them and the media prefer opposition and scandal. Fortunately, even if there is a strong propensity to make the whole thing a bundle, the political class is not all the same. The followers of populist democracy are a large part of it, but there is no shortage of the reactions of democratic opinion, even if the minimum threshold offered by good manners has reluctantly worn out, but this - it should be clear - is not a random effect. The risk of open conflicts was diminished, the reasons for showing themselves to be civil were paradoxically fallen, language was no longer remote, everything was divine, even that the recent past was just a heap of cheating. So politics became embarrassed and the exasperated competition produced by the democracy of alternation did the rest. The Constitution is still the fundamental law of the State. As all laws must be applied. It is a text that derives from its name particular nobility and authority and that has long been used by political actors as a pretext to find and hypothesize a shared future, at least that. Instead, political forces have decided to certify its obsolescence and in doing so, perhaps, it is they who derive the reasons for its greatest weakness. This brings us to the summary justice of a venerable institute such as the Senate of the Republic and the devices that were connected to it, so that, while political forces lack good manners and lack a common culture, there is also a text and a symbol that history had handed down to them, to which to refer. Can you ever be surprised that we are not only struggling to talk to each other, but we are even more struggling to govern and make ourselves understood by the citizens? The men may have been different for a thousand reasons, but a common denominator for most of them existed: they came from the classrooms of the classical high schools of the peninsula and perhaps their common frequentation with the pages of Cicero, Dante and Manzoni is not secondary. The constituents were men and women - unfortunately few - who had internalized certain patterns of behavior. They believed that in a representative Assembly we dress, act, speak in a certain way. That one can be ironic, sarcastic, polemic, but that never insults one another, which then is perhaps the most elementary trait of democratic coexistence. If it is true that shared political cultures exist only in the world of dreams, in political polemics to be democratic it is necessary to be, at least, well-educated. Thank you for your attention. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u109	A few days ago the Premier declared that seventy years have been trying to reform the Constitution. Well, apart from the fact that the Charter is sixty-seven years old, one thing is certain: I do not know if the Government will be able to bring home the reform, but surely this is the most clumsy attempt of the many who have been put on the field all these years. It is the worst for the type of reform that you want to introduce, for the ways and tools used, for the style, threats and blackmail that most have not hesitated to use. A home story page to forget that could offer Senator Zavoli a copious and certainly non-educational material for another episode of "The Night of the Republic." In fact, to look at the Renzi reform, nobody likes it, apart from his loved ones who, not to be sorry, dare not contradict it. The whole opposition doesn't like it; it doesn't like the vast sectors of the majority; it doesn't like the whole party either, because the mediation we're talking about is a mess, a compromise reached with Byzantine technicalities and an incomprehensible lexicon, with the aim more to safeguard the unity of the PD than to write a Constitution up to the country. Moreover, even a new member of the Premier in Parliament, like Senator D'Anna, in a reminiscence of modesty admits that he does not like the reform, indeed it sucks, and that his vote in favour has only one aim: to save the government. Even the reform has also agreed most of the commentators, from Scalfari to Ostellino, passing by the director of the "Corriere della Sera" (Corriere della Sera) Fontana, who even with different shades rejected or criticized it. A reform so stunned that it has seen sided on the same side of the barricade also characters that they have never loved, indeed they have hated, like Berlusconi, Grillo and Scalfari, that, starting from different points of view, they are convinced that the reform will determine an authoritarian turning point. On the other side Renzi, Alfano, Verdini, Cosentino, Auricchio and a Bersani in the dress of the misunderstood. A nice company, there is nothing but to say, that has two glue: the instinct of survival and the desire to share a little power. Such a situation would raise doubts for anyone. And anyone would have taken a pause for reflection. But not the Premier who, forgetting that constitutional reforms, by their nature, should spring from a parliamentary confrontation and collect a broad consensus (Napolitano and Mattarella even in 1995 presented a bill that introduced the two-thirds threshold for amendments to the Constitution), instead, transformed them into a point. A sort of hypertrophy of the ego, a brand-new Marquis of the Cricket of the Institutional Palaces who in comparison with Parliament recites the famous joke: "Because I know'me and you are not a... I'm gonna stop right here. Result: we are giving life to a Senate that at best will be useless, at worst harmful. I do not want to dwell on the authoritarian turning point I have already mentioned in the past, but certainly the combined reform and Italicum can even put a party of 25%, and its secretary, in the conditions of conquering the absolute majority, to choose the Head of State and condition the appointment of the majority of the judges of the Consulta; a scheme that recalls the Soviet system dominated by the PCUS. And it is useless that the Premier ironizes on the "authoritarian turn": Italicum has a single precedent in this country, the Acerbo law of the years of the beam. Anyway, I don't want to delay in historical controversy. I merely address the ideological basis of this reform, the so-called deciding democracy. Well, if Renzi and PD have arrived now, I wrote for more than thirty years, following the attempts of the many who tried to introduce it. But if before it made sense to bring this requirement into play, the Renzi government has shown that it is certainly not today's priority issue. The material Constitution, in fact, changed considerably after the Monti government and the tools used by the same Renzi government proves it: 45 trusts, 38 decrees-laws. In fact, on the case of the closure of the Colosseum, the Government in less than twelve hours was able to issue a decree-law to address the problem. Not to mention the abuse of delegated laws: there have been 17 such measures, between those approved and those in itinerant: that is, Parliament has been called on many times to adopt guidelines of a law that the Government will then implement as it pleases. Let us be honest: the Government has seen its prerogatives and powers extended to the detriment of Parliament. If he does not govern it is because he is not able to govern, certainly not because he lacks the powers. If anything, these changes, this increase in executive power did not respond to a process of greater legitimacy: just think that the government that is going to change the Constitutional Charter did not have any electoral baptism and does not do so on the basis of a mandate of voters, since the program of Bersani - to hear the interested person - was quite different. No, the philosophy that is being applied is different: instead of making the power of the Executive stronger and more legitimate through the direct election of the President of the Republic or the Premier, it tends to weaken Parliament. In short, it is not a strong government that draws its strength from the popular investiture to confront a strong Parliament, but a government that draws its strength from the weakening of Parliament. The arranged constitutional reform-Italicum, in fact, is the last stage of this process begun with the Monti Government with the blessing of the then head of the State, Giorgio Napolitano. In addition, this reform is not just following a false problem, but is likely to create another one: it also contains a recentralisation of powers. The State, to be clear, takes up the tasks it had delegated to the Regions. We have been witnessing the crisis of this local authority for years. There are many who theorize the birth of macro-regions that should replace the present. But while studying these operations and transformations, the Renzi Government creates a Senate made up of mayors and members of a list decided by candidates to the presidencies of the Regions. Of course, the proposal is confusing: it is not clear whether these senators will be elected directly or whether, instead, they will have to be invested by the Regional Councils. But apart from that, the real point is that these senators will be loyal to the governor who had them elected and of course defend their prerogatives with their teeth. So on the one hand the Government wants to invoke powers given to the Regions to itself; on the other hand, fatally, the idea of a Senate that risks becoming a union of the Regions advances. In short, with a certain superficiality, the foundations of tomorrow's institutional conflicts are being laid. We will repeat what happened with the reform of Title V and with the devolution. Only this time the institutional conflict, Chamber- Senate of the Regions, will be horizontal: there will be two bodies that will have the same degree of legitimacy, not to mention that in front of a government that sees its influence grow a lot, the main problem is not that of the relationship with the Regions, but that of creating an institution of guarantee. But can such a function be left to people who owe their election to governors? Would it not be better to accept the idea of a third guarantee institution? I say this with sincerity: if this is the mess we are moving towards, then I think it would be better, much better, to abolish the Senate altogether. I have tabled an amendment to that effect. One cannot make a constitutional reform having as a polar star - it is a litany of the Premier - only savings on wages. Such an objective cannot be a priority, but rather a consequence, also because we risk otherwise repeating the experience of the reform of the Provinces, which are still there and have the same costs as before. Let's face it all: if the goal is to save money, then it is necessary to rebalance the number of Members; six hundred and thirty are really too many for our country, a real scandal, so they should be reduced between the four hundred and the five hundred. I have also tabled amendments on this subject, because the only effective response against demagogy is, unfortunately, demagogy itself. As I said, I do not like this reform either because of the ways and instruments used: I did not like the atmosphere in this House and, above all, I found it inelegant, to use euphemism, the constant pressure exerted on President Grasso by the Government and its majority. A real stalking, culminated two days ago with the threats made by the Premier against the president of the Senate from the stage of a party meeting. Frankly, I was grounded. We are not talking here about the survival or not of a government - and even in such a case the forcing would be unacceptable - but even about the reform of the Constitutional Charter. Given that the form in this area is substance, everyone, but above all the Premier, we should ask ourselves whether we should not take a step back, because precedents of this kind can be a point of no return for our institutions. If this is the atmosphere in this House, i.e. on stage, in the backstage, that is, in the corridors of the Palace, the air is even worse. The newspapers describe a suk that is not very far from the truth, since new groups are born and those who until yesterday criticized the reform heavily, are now said ready to vote for it. The truth is that the interventions of constitutional reform should be discussed in an aseptic habitat, such as that of an operating room, far from conditioning of any kind; on the other hand, while on the stage we are talking about norms and principles, behind the scenes we are talking about Commission Presidencies or government fillings. All this is not only humiliating, but also grotesque: we have the feeling that this institution that we would like to reform, abolish, delegitimize and commit suicide on its own, because an institution lives not only of principles, rules and norms but, also and above all, of the dignity of its members and this is not Renzi's fault; if anything, it has in its political baggage a good deal of contempt and takes advantage of it. It is the fault of all those who sit in this House and have had a wrong approach to a task, a commitment that is among the highest to which an elected person can be called: to reform the Charter of Constitution of a country, of a people. It is a task that cannot be fulfilled by thinking of its own interest, dear D'Anna, Amoruso, Eva Longo, also because, in these fringes, the promises are often written on the water or you can take fireflies for lanterns. Those who vote in favour of the reform with the hidden intention of reaching the end of the parliamentary term may find that approval is the easiest shortcut to stop it. These are calculations that should be left out of this assembly: you cannot vote for a constitutional reform because you keep a family, dear Auricchio, nor can you vote for it for friendship or, dear Bondi, for grudges. Let's face it: our dignity as senators would be diminished, we would wrong our conscience and, above all, harm the country. [[Applause from Senator Amidei. Congratulations.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u110	Senator Orellana is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u111	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, to prepare for this speech, I found myself reading again what I had done in the general debate in July 2014. I must say that I was not surprised to see that a large part of what I mentioned in that speech is still valid today. The critical points of then are still present and, in the meantime, the passage to the Chamber has added others. It is therefore a profound reform, which affects many articles of the Constitution. First of all, what are the objectives? They have always been clear and, I would say, agreeable: the overcoming of equal bicameralism, the reduction of the number of parliamentarians, the abolition of the CNEL and the revision of Title V (as can be seen also in the over-impression watching the videos of our sessions). The reasons for these objectives are also clear: to speed up the legislative process, to better connect the territorial institutions with the State, to reduce the costs of politics and the disputes between the State and the Regions, due to the competing competences provided for in Title V. Does this reform achieve all these objectives? I'm sure you do. Where then are the problems, the criticalities, the doubts of this text? In achieving these goals we have had - using a real euphemism - unpleasant side effects that need to be corrected. We have time to do this and it has to be done. In overcoming the bicameralism of equality, the legislative functions of the future Senate have been reduced so much that they cause doubt that it still makes sense. Without frustrating the objective of overcoming equal bicameralism, changes can be made to both Article 1 and Article 10 of the Boschi bill to increase the functions of the future Senate. In this context, my amending proposals go in the direction of adding to the Senate the equal legislative function for the laws referred to Title I and Title II of Part I of the Constitution. It concerns, within the Part One, the rights and duties of citizens, Title I on civil relations and Title II on ethical and social relations. The examination of a second Chamber of such important issues is, I believe, essential for producing balanced legislation. In fact, I do not think that the speed in parliamentary approval always gives positive results. Sometimes the examination by another branch of Parliament enriches a text, allows a more careful analysis, produces a better result. Another role to be strongly assigned to the future Senate is the link between the State, the Regions and the European Union. The current text, as amended by the House, weakens this role. These interventions are indispensable in order to reduce the criticalities of this text. We move on to the composition of the Senate, which has been modified to grasp the dual purpose of better connecting the Regions with the State through a Senate composed mainly of regional councillors and to reduce the costs of politics through the disappearance of the compensation to the senators and above all their drastic reduction in number: result fully achieved, even if doubts persist both on the dual position (senator and regional councilor) and on the presence of local administrators, such as the mayors, in the future Senate, which will still maintain important legislative functions. The mayors are elected as directors and certainly not as legislators. To be a legislator does not correspond to the mandate received by its voters; this should already be enough to avoid this double role: to have those elected to be legislators are to betray the popular mandate. In addition, the electoral body that elects a mayor does not correspond to the one that elects the parliamentarians and regional councillors as it is also composed of foreigners as far as the Community is concerned. In Italy we have so far separated everything concerning laws, legislation and administration. In the first case, the laws are called to compete only the Italians (both with the elections to choose the parliamentarians and with the laws of popular initiative and referendums) while in the second case, the administration, also extends to the foreigners of the European Union. With the mayors-senators we will have a first case in which the two areas intersect: have we carefully assessed the consequences? I'm afraid not. In fact this circumstance can represent a vulnus so that a mayor can become senator and therefore legislator. Italy signed in 1992 (ratified in 1994 and in force since 1997) the Convention on the participation of foreigners in public life at local level, signed in Strasbourg in 1992 by the Council of Europe. It is a question of extending the rights of passive and active electorate to foreigners with certain characteristics (typically the duration of residence). Italy has ratified this convention, however, limiting the passive electorate to only foreigners of the European Union and to the office of city councilor, limiting the active electorate to only foreigners of the European Union. Italy has therefore amended Part C of this Convention (which concerns the active and passive electorate) but, nevertheless, the direction is that and probably, by reciprocity, Italy will have to extend to foreign citizens of the European Union all administrative offices, including the office of mayor and vice mayor that currently the law reserves only to Italian citizens. This will collide with the possibility of a mayor to become senator as it is unimaginable that a mayor- administrator-foreign (the possibility requested by Europe) can also be at the same time senator and legislator. To introduce the constraint that mayors will become senators (i.e. legislators) represents, from a European perspective, a concrete impediment to the extension of the participation of foreigners in local political life. It is therefore contrary to the spirit of the Convention already cited and contrary to the Treaty of Lisbon, which states in Article 22 (1): Any citizen of the Union residing in a Member State of which he is not a national shall have the right to vote and to stand as a candidate in municipal elections in the Member State in which he resides, under the same conditions as nationals of that State. The Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union, approved on 14 November 2000 by the European Parliament and proclaimed in Nice on 7 December 2000, also states in Article 40 (the right to vote and to stand as a candidate in municipal elections) that "Every citizen of the European Union shall have the right to vote and stand as a candidate in municipal elections in the Member State in which he resides, under the same conditions as nationals of that State." Finally, among the adverse reasons for the Senator mayor, it must be borne in mind that the incompatibility between the office of mayor, senator and president of the metropolitan city or wide area, that is to say, has not been introduced. We may theoretically have a person who is paid only to be mayor but will also, at the same time, be the senator and the president of the Province. If then the mayor of a small municipality is chosen whose allowance is not sufficient to be a full-time mayor, he will find himself continuing his professional activity and, simultaneously, playing the role of mayor, senator and provincial president. It would be, you know, a real Superman. I hope, therefore, that we can amend Article 2 by reviewing the composition of the Senate and removing the figure of the mayors senators. The decision to impose the indirect election of senators among regional councillors without any form of popular investiture seems almost inexplicable. I wonder: which of the objectives of the reform mentioned above is to be achieved? This question is for me unanswered. The only reason I have heard - in my humble opinion, a little weak - is that I do not want to give the future senators a full popular investiture because this would be poorly reconciled with the new Senate that will no longer be a political assembly. For the important legislative functions that remain in charge of the future Senate this motivation turns out - I repeat - weak. It is therefore necessary to intervene in order to increase the popular investiture of the senators, also considering that the alternative of the second-degree elections, as thought, will be rightly considered by our fellow citizens as a Senate decided at the table by four or five political leaders, with the aim of creating a Senate of the most faithful, or even advisors in search of personal protection that provides parliamentary immunity. Despite the possible technical difficulties associated with Article 2, I hope that we can take action on this essential point. For example, we could intervene by introducing the direct election of future senators by citizens, but by limiting the passive electorate to regional advisers only. To hear the press reports - but not, unfortunately, the debate in this House - it is towards this solution that it seems that the Government and the majority and minority of the Democratic Party are proceeding. Let me take off a pebble. Already at the first reading last year I proposed amendments to this effect: these are amendments 2.1373, 2.1374 and 2.1375 which were tabled and presented in the Chamber in July 2014. If one of these amendments had been adopted at the time, this provision would be already contained in Article 2 instead of being presented, as was the case this morning, as a great solution. Another point to consider is the weight of the Senate in future joint sessions with the Chamber of Deputies. As you know, Parliament meets in joint session to elect the President of the Republic, the five members of the Constitutional Court and the members of the Supreme Council of the judiciary. Currently the relationship between senators and deputies is one to two (three hundred and fifteen senators in front of six hundred and thirty MPs, excluding senators for life), while in the reform it is expected a report of about one to six (hundred senators in front of six hundred and thirty MPs). The weight of the senators will therefore certainly be less given the numbers. Little harm could be thought of but, considering the majority that will be created in the Chamber of Deputies, we can have a political force that, in fact, appoints five members of the Constitutional Court after having contributed decisively to the election of the President of the Republic. On this point too, it is therefore essential to intervene, for example by claiming that the election of the President of the Republic requires a broad agreement between all political forces and that the appointment of certain members of the Constitutional Court is the exclusive competence of the Senate. There are other points, perhaps more technical but not less important, to be changed in order to make concrete the possibility of senators to intervene in the legislative process. For example, the deadline of only ten days from the approval of the House of a legal text within which the Senate may request to amend it is too tight. Without fomenting legislative delays in contrast with the spirit of the reform, we could come to the most reasonable thirty-day forecast. Other points - or, better, ideas - of improvement appear here and there in the text and should not be underestimated. In conclusion, I would like to say a few words about the importance of this vote and the spirit that must inspire us all. I believe that no party discipline, no conditioning, no pressure should be accepted by us when deciding on the votes we are required to give. This is a vast and profound revision of our Constitution, which, if approved, will have its effects well beyond the contingent period we are experiencing. It is a great responsibility that falls on our shoulders and the only lighthouse for us must be only the merit of the measure, nothing else. The text that will be produced is what we need to evaluate and judge. That's all, I repeat, that's all. [[Applause from the Mixed Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u112	He's enlisted to speak Senator Piccoli. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u113	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, representatives of the Government, the constitutional revision undertaken has led - in the succession of parliamentary readings - as well as to overcoming the bicameralism of equality, to the design of a new division of legislative competences between the State and the Regions. There is no doubt that the two profiles - as has emerged during the in-depth debate in both branches of Parliament - are closely related, also bearing in mind that maintaining the balance between state unity and territorial diversity, between centre and periphery, require, from time to time, continuous adjustments according to a path that guarantees, at the same time, the needs of the State and the Regions. However, I cannot fail to point out that, especially after the changes made by the House, there are contradictions and very negative aspects in the text. The need to eliminate the confusion of the division of legislative powers between the State and the Regions, contained in Title V of the Constitution, was one of the main objectives of this reform. At a careful reading, however, I think I can say that the "transfer" of matters, some in the exclusive competence of the State and others in the exclusive competence of the Regions, has been done in a confused, contradictory way. The first concern raised by the reading of the new Article 117 is that of the multiplicity and variety of terms used to indicate the legislative competences exclusively state and those referring to regional power: "fundamental principles," "coordination rules," "general principles," "general ordinal profiles"; vague and generic terms, inconsistent with each other, which will create even more conflicts of competence than they will manage to resolve. The initial objective, which can be shared, of a profound simplification of the division of competences between the State and the Region in the end has failed. To introduce in the exclusive legislative power of the State the novelty of the "general and common provisions" and to allow the law of the State, on proposal of the Government, to intervene in matters not reserved to the exclusive legislation means to create even more interpretative doubts on the boundaries of the legislative competences, nullifying the plan of simplification of the division of competences between State and Regions. This regulatory disorder is the inevitable consequence of a hasty and imprudent way of proceeding. For example, more serious consideration seems to be given to the fact that certain subjects and services for citizenship have characteristics that can only be carried out effectively by the regional entity. In fact, a virtuous Region agency can and must deal with transport, social services, health and agriculture. The undeniable diversity of quality in the services rendered to citizens between the different Italian Regions, essentially between North and South, cannot lead to the exclusion of the Region as such of these prerogatives. On the contrary, it should be a challenge for the whole Republic so that the virtuous experiences are spread and the good practices adopted throughout the Italian territory. Are we really sure, ladies and gentlemen, that with the institutional set-up resulting from this reform, will competing competences finally be an old memory? To me it seems that the main characteristic of the new Senate, the representation of territorial institutions, the backwardness of its competences and the disorganic formulation of the new distribution of legislative competences inevitably lead to further inconsistencies and normative illogicality. Then there is another element to be taken into account. The emptying of the powers of the Senate resulting from the changes made by the Chamber of Deputies to Articles 1 and 10 (the 55 and 70 of the Constitution respectively) draws a weak Senate, never autonomous from the Chamber: a secondary Chamber. This element further aggravates the picture, because the spirit with which this reform has been conceived, and on which there is almost total agreement, is that the Senate must represent the authorities of the territories and their control, in some matters, over the work of the State. But if the Senate itself sees this indispensable function deeply weakened, the combined arrangement is a serious limitation of regional functions, either directly, through the changes to Article 117, or indirectly, through, precisely, the irrelevance of the Senate that should represent them. Indeed, doubts in this regard have been raised during the hearings of various constitutionalists. The draft law also provides for a safeguard or supremacy clause, where the State can intervene by law (on a proposal from the Government) also in regional matters, to protect values attributable to unity or national interest. The introduction of the safeguard or supremacy clause, aimed at guarding the national interest, is, in my view, more likely to penalise the legislative competence of the regions. Most of the necessary unitary needs are already guaranteed by the allocation to the State of the exclusive subjects of Article 117, and this clause would also leave the choice of when and how to intervene only and exclusively in the hands of the Government, causing a predictable centralization towards the State of also the competences of the Regions, thus reducing their legislative power, frustrating the objective of simplification and eliminating the boundaries of the division of the different competences. The remodulation of the functions between State and Regions needed to revisit Article 117 by redefining the division of competences so as to reduce, if not eliminate as far as possible, interpretative doubts, contradictions between norms, while helping to give greater expression, representation and capacity to respond to those that are local needs. In summary, we are faced with a strong compression of territorial autonomy on the one hand, through the reduction of competences and, on the other hand, through the imposition of a supremacy clause, in the direction of a drained Senate of its powers for the benefit of a system clearly centralized of the constitutional order, in which the Government would continue to play a role of absolute primacy. The idea that there is an absolute first state is, in my opinion, a serious contradiction of the principle of subsidiarity that the Constitution itself protects. Then there is another aspect that raises many doubts. I refer to the amendment introduced at first reading in the Chamber of Deputies concerning the provision contained in Article 39 that for the regions with special autonomy and the autonomous provinces of Trento and Bolzano the changes to Title V provided for in this text will apply only after the revision of the respective Statutes. To introduce this forecast confirms once again that the Government forgets all those territories that today suffer the unequal treatment generated by the recognition of certain forms of autonomy to specific Regions. In fact, if it is certainly acceptable that, in Article 31 of this Constitutional Review Law, we are concerned to remove the economic and social imbalances, in order to promote economic development, cohesion and social solidarity, to provide, with paragraph 12 to Article 39, that the statutory adjustment takes place on the basis of agreements with the Regions with Special Statute and with the Autonomous Provinces means to grant, once again, the possibility to strengthen the territorial autonomy, enhancing the aspects of substantial differentiation, that is, the legislative and fiscal capacity of each Region. This expresses yet another dramatic indifference towards those citizens and territories, contiguous to these Regions with special status, suffering from unequal treatment and opportunity. Let me say that the introduction of this provision is logical, allowing, in fact, the regions with special status - and we understand the reason - and the Autonomous Provinces to negotiate in the second place additional forms of relief. In this way, the safeguard or supremacy clause - which Article 120 of the Constitution refers to as applicable in the Regions with special autonomy - is conditional, as far as its exercise is concerned, on what will subsequently be decided in the revision of the legal sources presiding over the same special autonomy. This is an indicator of a weak government, unable to resolve economic and social inequalities. The process of constitutional reform was a good opportunity to give a response to those territories, already disadvantaged by their economic, social and physical characteristics, mostly contiguous with territories that enjoy particular and beneficial forms of autonomy that aggravate an already complex situation. Who will allow this opportunity to be lost in nothing will have to assume the responsibility to explain to those territories and to all citizens the sense of legitimizing a profound difference in performance and opportunities - which is called "competitive advantage" - between different areas of the national territory and, at the same time, of banning the principles of equality and guarantee instrumentally. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII]]

Here the fate of the country is entrusted to the arbitrariness of a minority that becomes a majority for artificial reinforcements and not for the consents of the citizens. This creates an imbalance of legitimacy between the Premier and the Assembly, which translates into supremacy of the executive power over the legislative and indirectly also over the judicial order. The three fundamental powers of a democracy are definitely out of balance. That today it is considered normal, that we take it almost for granted, that those who withdraw from the index are the confirmation that the Italian public debate is sick; that already on the agenda of discussion, even before in the solutions, there is a dangerous distortion of principles and values. An artificial constitutional emergency has been built to give political legitimacy to a government without direct electoral mandate. It's yet another Italian anomaly. In a normal country, the government does not deal with the Constitution. In a normal country the Executive governs according to a program presented to voters. You can derogate from these simple rules in extraordinary situations and for a short time; from us the exceptional state will last for almost all this decade. You can't just blame the last ones who came. For twenty years the Constitution has been changed for political purposes: first the CentroSinistra with Title V, then Berlusconi in 2005, Fini with the ius sanguinis of voting abroad, Tremonti's attempts on Article 41. All constitutional reforms failed, because wrong was the method. But already in the 1980s, when the parties lost the country's power of government, they took the habit of saying that it was not their fault, but the Constitution. In order not to face the crisis of politics they have opened a crisis of the institutions; they have begun to browse the atlas of the French, English, American, Spanish and German models. But institutional perfectism is a symptom of the illness of politics; healthy Constitutions are imperfect because they are produced by history. The American decision-making model is crazy; it is devoid of the decree-law, yet it ruled an empire. The imperfections are compensated by the political will, which is like the courage of Don Abbondio: those who do not have it cannot give it. For thirty years the Italian political class, instead of ruling the country, has consoled itself with the teddy bear of institutional reforms. When President Renzi boasts of doing things scheduled for twenty years, he does not realize that he speaks as a conservative. My generation has not been up to the task. The sad news is that not even the generation after us seems capable of doing so. This legislature - the seventeenth - certainly has an unexpected and revolutionary feature compared to the previous ones: I refer to the presence of the 5 Star Movement. Everyone - political parties, media, lobbying, strong powers - reacted to his break-in into the institutions as if it were to be mourned. The elaboration of mourning in this Parliament and in the country, especially in those who hold the reins of information and real power, has developed according to five phases that are precisely those that describe psychoanalysis. The first is that of denial or refusal; then that of anger; to follow that of the plea; then depression and, finally, acceptance of the mourning itself. The mourning, arrived after the presence of the 5 Star Movement in the institutions, consists in the awareness that there is an end in this Parliament and, probably, in the country, understood as it once meant: the end of the system of party domination, first of all, and the end of what I consider to be the real arranged combination that could lead - indeed, it is leading - the country to ruin. I am not referring to what is normally mentioned in this House, but to the combined arrangement of a system of power that for decades imposed and controlled everything and everyone and the action of the media, which have been and continue to be constantly at the service of the system of power. This is indeed a dangerous combination for democracy and this is what has produced, above all, the second phase of the elaboration of mourning, the stage of anger, to which all have adapted, especially with the new political force entered into the institutions: the 5 Star Movement, precisely. In the process of mourning, here in this House and in the political groups, it is above all the majority groups, and above all here in the Senate, that have taken forward, also with respect to the President of the Council Renzi and the Government that he leads. Those of the Dem minority are well within the fifth stage of the mourning process, the passive acceptance phase.
