ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u129	Madam President, to present the choice of reforming the institutions and the second part of the Constitution as a requirement of a party, of the Democratic Party or of the majority, dictated by obscure reasons or worse by an irresistible authoritarian vocation, means falsifying reality. I have heard in this noble and important discussion references to the history of our country and to the events that marked the work of the Constituent. But in the face of a careful and living memory for what happened seventy years ago, rightly attentive and alive, I notice a difficulty in remembering what happened only two and a half years ago. After an electoral result that had not produced a clear majority of government, and above all in the face of the obvious crisis of relationship between citizens and politics, the growing distrust in the institutions, I recall that, in the face of the sharpening of a real crisis of our democracy, Parliament, or at least its vast majority, has considered to make this legislature a legislature dedicated to reforming the institutions to rebuild the relationship between citizens and democracy. We are doing what President Napolitano called us to after his re-election and to whom President Mattarella called us in his inauguration speech. We are doing what all political forces declare necessary, what so many majorities have tried unnecessarily in the past. This is why we are having this debate, knowing that it is not enough, that we need to change politics and parties in order to rebuild the relationship of trust with the citizens, and we are also doing this, but above all we need to put the institutions in a position to give answers to the citizens, we need to modernise the institutions, because look, if the pact between citizens and the state that is in crisis today is broken, if the State appears to the citizens as an obstacle and not as a resource, we risk that the words that I have heard here repeatedly mention with passion, such as democracy and freedom, lose their sense. Precisely in the choice made two years ago to make this legislature a constituent legislature is the reason why it is not strange, let alone illegitimate the intervention of the government. On the other hand, here too, looking at the facts, in the face of 134 amendments to the text approved in the previous two readings, the accusations of those who accuse Government and majority of closure and arrogance are obviously instrumental. We have tried and are trying to achieve a broad consensus and this text, which was approved by a larger majority than the Government at the last reading, demonstrates this. The same choice to ask citizens to speak in the referendum should reassure those who are presenting this reform as an undemocratic coup d'état. Much has been said, but I think it would be useful to come back to a few points in the debate. There has been a discussion in which many have spoken of an authoritarian turning point, of a system that puts everything in the hands of the Premier. That is not the case. This reform does not put a hand in the form of a government. This is the difference from the 2005 reform. Here there is no enlargement of the powers of the President of the Council and I find it amazing that Forza Italia and Lega continue to insist on this point, hiding the fact that their reform of 2005, which was not at all the same as what we are discussing, strengthened the powers of the Premier, until it gave him the power to dissolve the Chambers and to command and no longer coordinate the Ministers. So, no authoritarian breakthrough, no high and strong Premier. But, it is said, the will to put only one man in command and the undemocratic turn is that with Italicum 60% of the Members will be appointed, forgetting that the issue is another, that over two thirds of the Members of the majority, whatever it may be, will be elected with preference and chosen by the citizens. There is no reason to shout at the attack on democracy, unless it is considered undemocratic that in a country there is the possibility for a majority to exist and govern, to give continuity to government action. Then I would advise you to avoid this torment that we want to prevent voters from voting, I say that for those who use it. The citizens in this country vote for the municipalities, the regions, the Parliament and I could add the circumscription councils and the European Parliament. This argument is not really credible. I believe that in the next few days we will be able to further improve the balance between powers and the system of guarantees, but on this I want to make an observation perhaps against the current with respect to many interventions. The Senate of the self-governments that we are building will be stronger, will weigh more, not only and not so much because its members will be chosen by the citizens, but especially because its members will be representatives of the territorial institutions, will have the strength of the regional institutions and the municipalities behind them throughout their mandate. This will give weight and function to the Senate. Look, beyond the folklore of the 82 million amendments, beyond the imaginative behindological reconstructions that we heard yesterday and today, in these months we are having an important debate, we are doing it here and in the country, certainly also in the parties. I say that I am proud to be in a party that discusses; a normal party that does what every party should do: if we discuss it is not a permanent congress. You are right: it is not an internal matter of PD, but precisely for this reason we debate and compare ourselves, we are in a plural party that discusses and decides looking at the interest of the country, we take care of this, do it yourself. See if dissent is not expressed by divisions, as is the case with Forza Italia, or is not conculcated with expulsions. Try, look for solutions and synthesis as we do. This does not mean that there are parliamentarians blackmailed and vexed, spineless, courtiers, we do not need your solidarity. There are parliamentarians who are in a party and freely share a reform project for the country and discuss and seek synthesis when there are differences. To insult those who do not agree with you, to accuse those who are mostly attached to the chair or courtiers, non-free persons, is the path of those who have no arguments and prefer to delegitimize the opponent rather than discuss the matter. You explained to us that we were servants and courtiers when we voted for the jobs act or other measures, each time we supported the reforms of this government. In reality we have worked for the country, not for ourselves, and the data of these months on the economy and the job [[Comments from the M5S Group]] , turn it the way you want it, show it: 250,000 more jobs, growth data, the recovery of domestic consumption and exports are data that we should all be pleased with - and some people are sorry - and that they are also a little fruit of our work. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u132	Mr President, representatives of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to anticipate the conclusions that I shall come to at the end of my speech: I prefer the abolition of the Senate tout court rather than this reform, which does nothing but empty it of powers and functions. Mine is not a closing position towards reforms, on the contrary: I voted in favour of this draft constitutional law at the first passage in the Senate, following the indications of my Group. However, the draft constitutional revision that I voted for has been completely upset by the House; I therefore find myself fully in agreement with the contrary position of Forza Italia. I have always believed that in order to best represent the cross-section of civil society, thus guaranteeing continuity and validity over time, reforms to the Constitutional Charter should include broad sharing. On the contrary, the Democratic Party, in violation of the principle of democracy contained in its name, discusses the reform within the seats of the party and not in the Chamber, thus not guaranteeing dialogue and confrontation with the other groups representing the Italian people. We know that the deadline for tabling amendments was 9 a.m. this morning; should we wait for the media to know them? The PD, it is well to remember, governs having obtained 25,43 percent of the votes in the Chamber in 2013 and having obtained the majority prize thanks to the 3.2 percent of SEL, which is now firmly in opposition; a majority prize thanks to which the text of reform of the Constitution has been overturned in the House, which is now being imposed on the Senate in an almost armored text. Forza Italia already in 2005, during the Berlusconi Government, had proposed a constitutional reform that was approved by both Chambers and that subsequently did not pass the proof of the confirmation referendum. The text adopted provided for the reduction of parliamentarians (one third less than today), the reduction of legislative time and, finally, the specialization of the roles of the two chambers. The Senate, therefore, was recognized as functions and tasks quite different from those envisaged today by the reform under consideration. The same would have participated with dignity in the election of the President of the Republic and exercised control over the work of the Chamber of Deputies. The current reform, on the other hand, presents a strong reduction, if not almost an annulment, of the functions of the Senate and also with an invisible power, reducing the number of senators from three hundred fifteen to one hundred and maintaining the six hundred thirty MPs; it is evident to all the enormous disproportion. The emptying of the functions and powers of the Senate, with the centralization of the same to the Chamber, should have foreseen at least a greater devolution towards the Regions, instead the latter have undergone a strong reduction of their competences; they have been depleted of their power, so much so that the bill provides for a supremacy of the state competences and therefore a centralization of the powers in the Chamber, therefore an institutional neocentralism. It destroys the traditional architrave that recognized roles similar to the two Chambers and comes to a de facto monocameralism, where the equal participation of the two Assemblies will be limited to the laws of constitutional revision and to a reduced number of ordinary bicameral laws. The Senate will remain a power of veto that will be inconsistent on the laws passed by the House, considering that this can overcome the same veto or any changes proposed by Palazzo Madama with a simple majority vote, and let's remember that that simple majority would be the same determined by the majority prize of Italicum. Moreover, with the same numbers determined by Italicum through the arranged combined, the House of six hundred thirty members, compared to the Senate of only one hundred members, would elect the judges of the Constitutional Court, the President of the Republic and more. Personally and as an exponent of the Forza Italia party, I believe that the regulatory framework proposed by us in 2005 is still more valid than the one proposed by the reform under discussion. Colleagues, with the reform of Minister Boschi we are witnessing the birth of a Chamber of Deputies which are attributed not powers but overpowers and a Senate of the Republic with residual powers, or rather functions, because there is no talking about powers. I think it is also worth stressing the new composition planned for the Senate: mayors and regional councillors who do not know on what basis they will be chosen, but it can be assumed that the choice will be of a political type and expression of the majority of the governing party. Colleagues, it is clear to all of us what commitment we are required to play the role of senators. It is equally clear to many of us what the commitment required of a mayor or regional councillor to play the role for which he was elected is. I therefore ask you how it is possible to play two such challenging roles at the same time; in doing so, there is a risk that neither one or the other will be done well. I also believe that I can say that citizens would not be satisfied with having a mayor or regional councillor who, having to be present in Rome to carry out his parliamentary activity, would not have the opportunity to best safeguard the interests of their territory; unless it is a Senate emptied of functions, as we believe it will, and therefore the presence of future senators in Rome is completely superfluous. One could arrive at the French paradox, which provides for the vote by proxy required by the repeated absences of the senators, elected precisely among the mayors. In France, however, a constitutional reform is being considered which elects senators without further assignments; exactly the opposite of what we are going to do today with this constitutional reform. Ladies and gentlemen, the lack of presence in the House during the reform debate confirms the awareness that the text is armored and that any changes concern only the debates between majority and minority within the Democratic Party. Their greatest concern would appear to be that of the elective system, which I am also sincerely uninterested in. Since the Senate will no longer function, I wonder if these really are the conditions to keep a Chamber stripped of any competence and power, left as the facade of a fake bicameralism, and if it is not more just to eliminate it, thus realizing the real cost cut of politics so advertised by Prime Minister Renzi and included in the title of the reform. We are faced with the drift of democracy, where the citizen is no longer placed at the centre of political decisions, but at the extremes. A policy that, if this reform should pass, will regulate with power firmly in the hands of the Government, or rather its President not directly elected by the people, the return to a pseudodictatorship. Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, this is what we are talking about. I conclude with a reflection: this reform in fact only strengthens the power of the Government at the expense of a Parliament, a true exponent of democracy and the sovereignty of the people, further motivation for my vote against the constitutional reform under consideration. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII. Congratulations.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u134	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, on the one hand, the stunt of the debate in the Commission, on the other hand, the mockery of tabling 81 million amendments put an end to this painful reading by the Senate on the substantive issue surrounding the defence of democracy.And yet, in the end, the Groups in the Commission had withdrawn many of the amendments tabled, thinking of dealing with the debate there and leaving on the table only the most significant texts and those of real interest to the citizens (here we are, the citizens); we have tabled few of them of quality, all of which are geared towards those citizens. On the other hand, the permanent congress of the Democratic Party which has been going on for about a year on the media and unfortunately also in Parliament - and I remind you above all, Mr Mirabelli - distorts the natural debate on reforms, which should see the whole House of this Senate, like that of the House, engaged in a substantial unity of intent in modernising the form of government and form of representation. The contribution of the Conservatives and Reformists, through our proposals for amendments, is in fact linked to the issues closest to the lives of citizens. The issue of taxation is for us the main concern of the citizens, together with the absence of work and the economic crisis. It is the point we hold most, the point we ask for the Constitution to safeguard. We call for the Constitution to safeguard the need for the overall tax levy to never exceed two fifths of GDP in the previous year. It would be a revolution, this yes, indeed it would be the revolution, the approval of this amendment would force everyone, all politics to review the entire tax system of our country, universally recognized as oppressive. For us, in fact, the time has come to act, to bully the hand brake of the tax burden on the country. It is one of our battles, which will not end here: let's also say that less taxes for all, as we have always argued, must be clearly written in the Constitution, so that governments do not derogate to the fundamental principle of Article 53, which wants the citizen to contribute only according to his own possibilities and today those possibilities are scarce. In addition to the tax ceiling, we ask to include rules that protect the certainties of the Italians and that, recalling the statute of the taxpayer's rights, put the citizen at the centre of the law with his right to live in his own country as such and not as a subject. We have taken care to address equally sensitive and important issues for the country such as the infrastructural equalization, an apparently difficult expression, but for us the country must be held together in development, not only between North and South, but also between North and North and between South and South. For us, too, the direct election by the citizens of the President of the Republic, if Parliament is unable to elect it by the fifth ballot, serves to fill the vacuum of latent democracy that this reform produces. The revision of the regional structure also compensates for the heavy cost of providing essential services to citizens, where many regions have dramatically failed. Let it be clear that we, too, Conservatives and Reformists - as my colleague Mr Floris said earlier - in the light of reforms that have become "reformettes," prefer the real simplification of the abolition of the Senate, so as not to end this ugly parliamentary page in the same way as that of the Provinces. I would like to give back to this majority what not yet President Napolitano said in 2005 on the reform of the Berlusconi Government: a horrendous constitutional reform. Someone, moreover, had already predicted this. Indro Montanelli said that only the Constitution can be changed in Italy; everything else remains as it is. I, however, do not want to give in to pessimism and then wonder who can change things. The answer is simple, Madam President, always the citizen. Is it possible, Mr Quagliariello and colleagues from the People's Area Group (NCD-UDC), to whom I address this reflection, that this principle, which is only the citizen who has the power to change, both on the reforms and on Italicum, has escaped you so much to generate today, almost a year later, so many crocodile tears? At the half funeral of democracy that is being celebrated today we do not ask our repentant colleagues for this choice to bring flowers, but only for opposing votes. For us, the citizen must always be the protagonist of political and institutional life and, when this is difficult, he must be promoted and supported. That is why one of the amendments we have tabled concerns the issue of primaries which, if properly regulated, allow us to become, like other countries, a modern democracy. We want to open the door to all citizens who want to participate directly in our democratic life: why, instead, do you do everything to get that citizen, the blessed citizen, from whom democracy is promoted? Dear colleague Paolo Romani - here near me, today repented of the position he expressed a year ago with many colleagues from Forza Italia - I am sure you remember how only a year ago we begged you not to trample on all the common history of the center-right, our glories, our dreams and our values, melting you in that Lion pact of the Nazarene. The oxen, ladies and gentlemen, despite the protests of someone - like Mr Gasparri, whom I listened to this morning - must not escape from the stable. The oxen, ladies and gentlemen, have already escaped and are exposed in the butcher shop around the corner. But I want to respond to this with an appeal because, in my opinion, there is the time and the way to remedy it. Let us start again while we are in time with a strong vote against, not to be remembered as the last democratic Parliament that, before committing suicide, has produced a legal abortion: a reform that intervenes on everything, as if it were an omnibus decree, except on what really matters, namely the future and the well-being of the citizens. I appeal to you, Madam President, to send it to President Grasso, who I ask not to be passively involved in the compulsory winding-up of democratic representation. I ask President Grasso to defend the vote of the Italians, to protect the Italians from the tension produced by a non-representative Senate and an overwhelming electoral law. Democracy can and must be reorganized with modern processes, to make it more effective and real, but it cannot be distorted and distorted at the service of a man and, worse still, at the service of a party. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u136	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, the debate these days has also made it clear, if we still need it, that the main objective of this reform is the overcoming of equal bicameralism. I do not consider it to be an absolute truth, given that parliamentary experience has also taught us that often the two readings allow to improve a regulatory text and perhaps to read in a better perspective also its functionality. But so much, the time seems ripe to deal with this novelty. Most Members of this House, even from what we have heard these days, are now clearly geared towards this objective. It is a major position in this direction that at least limits, if you want to look at the glass half full, the danger of coming out of trace in what remains a delicate process of constitutional reform that you wanted to start. If there is therefore a common point from which to start, a contact channel to find a useful compromise in Parliament for the country, that is without doubt. That is also an objective that should embrace the ambition of the citizens, who want a more streamlined legislative and democratic process, it is true, but who have also lived on their own the experience of the last twenty years in which many legislators, in the name of simplification, have produced interventions that have had completely opposite effects. The famous reform of Title V of the Constitution is the most obvious example: a reform that was born badly and quickly that today forces even those who were most solicitous supporters to admit serious problems that should be absolutely correct. In order to be more recent, we have the example of the abolition of the Provinces, which has produced problems and problems on which this House too has often been involved. That is why I believe that citizens are not passionate about a debate that is entangled in technicalism and that instead makes it a bit confusing merit and risks that accompany this reform system, which is born with an anomaly almost congenital in this legislature. We must not forget that this reform has also been given birth by the Commission and that unfortunately it still tends to be managed, more than in Parliament, by the majority or perhaps only by the relative majority party in this country. I believe that we cannot passively go along with a process of reform that is packaged and sealed with the government's own label: it would be a serious mistake as well as an inappropriate invasion of the field. My inspiration - it has always been, but today it is even more so - is that this remains a noble passage, the noblest, so noble as to make the veins tremble to the wrists of us legislators, as some colleagues said before me. We are amending our Constitutional Charter and this cannot and must not remain an exercise to the sole majority, but it must represent a moment, a test in which we must all feel the responsibility for a delicate task and commit ourselves to giving the best in order not to betray the values of the Italian Constitution and to give a more functional system than the current one and the needs of the country. That is why I find it frankly embarrassing that muscle tests should be used to try to bring home results from the majority, certainly, and, for obvious reasons of countermeasure, from the opposition. In this sense, while I still have the greatest respect for the approach that some colleagues have legitimately chosen to deal with the process of this reform, since I considered it highly spoiled, I wanted to try to relaunch, trying at this stage to escape a merely obstructive approach. I am not excited by an extended arm-arm with amendments; the risk is that of wielding a weapon, expressing, yes, an extraordinary force, but that in the end, given the practice, already adopted several times in this legislature, of using methods that inhibit amending obstructionism, such as, for example, the so-called kangaroo. There is more. The value of a majestic amending activity certainly lends itself alongside the alibi of procedural counter-measures, thus reducing the responsibility to confront itself in order to add something positive to a reform process. I do not want to lend myself to this alibi; I do not want to lend myself to the alibi of making parliamentary dialogue less easy, while at the same time risking irritating and disrespecting the citizens, who are now tempted to brand these initiatives as parliamentary melines. For this reason, I have decided to table a few amendments and all of them in substance: a challenge and in the same way an approach of responsibility which, I hope, however, is reflected in this House. Today, in fact, we are called to focus on the merit, not wanting to consider the opportunity to make a contribution of value and positive significance to the reform process as yet completely disappeared. If we still have a large number of authoritative constitutionalists calling on us to correct this reform and to make clear some of its fundamental steps, then obviously the debate on the amendments that must take place in the next few days must be the best opportunity for us to reverse the process that has characterized the process so far. We cannot, in fact, allow the passage on the amendment of the Constitution to be only the ownership of the parties that support the Government, leaving to the opposition the role and the task of a mere obstructive activity. If we agree at least on this - and I think it could not be otherwise - then there are two levels of intervention that we must take, in my view, at the same time. The first is of substance. The reflection that we must make is on the new functions and powers to be assigned or, better, hand over to the new Chamber that will come to life after this reform, in order to make sure that the bicameralism is truly equal and there are, however, unnecessary and harmful overlaps with other organisms. What are the guarantees and instruments with which to ensure the right and democratic balance between powers? That is the question to which we are called to answer. I believe that today, in the current plant under discussion, this answer is not exhaustive. But it's not enough. We want to simplify with respect to prefect legislative bicameralism, then, however, we expect as many as four, if not five, separate legislative procedures, that it is easy to predict will give rise to frequent and not easily resoluble conflicts between the two Chambers on who does what. Or again, where do we want to start for a fair redistribution of competences between the various parts of the state? In my opinion, for example, the wordings produced so far relating to the distribution of legislative competence are not sound. I refer to the so-called general and common provisions, which unfortunately already generate uncertainty, as reported in the case of the Senate Studies Service, and which are therefore intended to produce further conflict between central and peripheral levels of government. In summary, the concurrent competence, which has generated so much litigation at the Constitutional Court, comes out the door, but would come back from the window. In short, we risk carrying with us, as an undesirable legacy, a vice that we already know and of which we pay heavily the price, even in the most recent and lower-ranking measures (I refer, for example, to the unblocking Italy). In order to remain in fact to the recent facts, we know that in these days some referendums will take place in order to avoid the danger of the search for hydrocarbons through drilling in the Adriatic, a decision taken by the central government, to which the Regions are now forced to oppose demanding the referendum repeal. Another theme: the focus of the discussion remains the electivity of the senators, therefore the exercise of democracy as defined by definition, that is the right-duty of citizens to elect their representatives. The message that accompanied this year of discussion has always been that the non-elective Senate becomes necessary to overcome equal bicameralism. I believe that this is a wrong message and that I hope this parliamentary debate will finally be able to heal. The second level, on the other hand, is of form, a form that summarizes and summarizes the whole scope of this measure, because here the reflection and deepening to be done is on the index of democracy that we want to achieve through the election of representatives, who will then be called to play their role on these very benches of ours. By permanently attaching this reform to the new electoral law, we must produce the effort to predict its overall result, resulting from both changes, so important for the institutional architecture of our country. It is necessary to avoid the risk of possible influence by the Commission on the other powers of the State, first and foremost President of the Republic and Consulta. It is a risk that becomes even more dangerous if we think that with Italicum the Executive is handed over to a single political force, because of a choice that, in my opinion, has to be discussed again. It is a choice that assigns the majority prize to the list and therefore to a single party, not instead to the coalition. We are still in time to correct the course, as Italicum exposes us to the risk of an authoritarian drift. The choice to assign the majority prize to a single list and the possibility, for now only apparently averted, to see senators elected through forms of second degree risk concentrating the choices of representatives in the intimacy of party secretariats, and this is both in the House and in the Senate. It is a choice that proposes, under a new guise, perhaps even more daring, a solution that the Consulta has not delayed to define illegitimate. The time, however, Madam President, as always on these occasions, will manifest itself, referring to the need to conclude without being able to develop certain issues, which also, in my opinion, are relevant to the issues of rights guarantees or the limitation of regional functions. I hope that the debate on the amendments will not be forced and will give us this opportunity, because I believe it will give us a political conclusion to a legislative process capable of widening the audience and not keeping it close to the parties that support the majority of the Government. We need a supplement of constituent animus, said some colleagues, to seek solutions shared even in perspective from the celebration of a referendum, which cannot be transformed, in any way, into a plebiscite, but must represent a free popular convocation, which remains free from evidence of force. In short, let's make sure that these days is an effective work, open, and that it leaves spaces of intervention able to make us fulfill the responsibility to deliver to those who will come after us a constitutional system so modified, but able to stand and in balance, and perhaps more functional than the present. Simplification tout court cannot, in my opinion, be a recipe for achieving this important objective. [[Applause from Senator Petraglia.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u140	Madam President, Minister, Undersecretaries, ladies and gentlemen, before I can even know the opinion of the President of the Senate on what is actually amendable, the aim I aim at with the course of the speech is to promote the focus of the most thorny issues after the vote of the Chamber of Deputies. Our regionalism is not necessarily to be put on the shields, but perhaps it does not deserve to be punished because the reform of 2001, which has made it neglect, has already laid the premises for some incongruent capitals. As for the first inconsistencies, the rule contained the distinction between what is of the State and what is of the Regions, or of competing legislation. It is now intended to remedy with this passage and we tend, therefore, to leave only between State and Regions, but the new constitutional vocabulary makes a reference to general and common provisions that will cost us another ten years of conflict before the Constitutional Court to understand who should do what. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] I really call everyone to our common sense, because a serious problem that we have had as a result of amendments to the Constitution out of an overall view is linked to the fact that, by simply making semantic translations of political difficulties, in the end they remain on the ground of the issues just mentioned and not substantially modified by the new provisions; issues that then require a confrontation - I would say a competition - on which are going to give the best of themselves the most equipped regions and with the best legislative offices, while those less equipped and with less adequate legislative offices inevitably remain behind. Attention. When I talk about the problem and the danger to democracy, I do not intend to evoke in an absurd way imaginative risks, but rather to specify the fact that, where the institutions are made weaker because the task they are called is less clear and the tools and opportunities they have available to achieve it, democracy is inevitably more fragile. And it is a major problem because, when we make it possible to force a new role of the Government and, in particular, of the President-in-Office of the Council in the scheme that we imagine, it is clear that, if we do not have to oppose this reading an adequate interpretation of what the democratic system will produce through electoral law, we highlight a further fragility of the system. Of course, the most problematic observations have been heard on this, even those which are the result of greater political tension. In fact, the problem of coherence is really very simple. A system within which, if there is a strengthening of the role of the Government and the Head of the Executive, it is necessary to understand how the other orders and powers of the State are equipped in order to compete, compete together (cumpetere) and together seek the best solution to solve the problems of our country. Furthermore, the third question concerns the risk of a democratic deficit caused by the massive majority prize for the party, plus the ballot, plus the forced composition of the Senate. And here are two assets that - from my point of view - are interesting. The problem, in fact, is not the elective Senate. The problem is what the Senate should be. And what the Senate should be is understood even more when it is claimed that it is the Senate of the autonomous and, paradoxically, it leaves the State-Regions Conference the place of negotiation between the State and the Regions. If the Senate is not the Bundesrat, if the Senate wants to be something different from some kind of post-rail job, what really must it do and how is the instrument of election proportionate for the system to work? And confidence - as I said just now - after this change, in fact would no longer go from Parliament to the Government, but from the Head of Government to Parliament. In this way the Senate would become a completely unspoken organ, without real autonomy, while the House of Deputies would be subject, by virtue of a huge majority prize, to the hegemony of a single party, in the context of a drastic reduction of popular representativeness. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u141	Madam President, I begin by pointing out that in this parliamentary term, even before the late Nazarene Pact, four main areas of constitutional change had been identified by the Commission's investigation into the reforms, the implementation of which was unanimously considered necessary: firstly, the reduction in the number of parliamentarians, the overcoming of equalism and a stricter discipline of the decree of urgency; secondly, the strengthening of the prerogatives of the Government in Parliament, through the single chamber trust, and the simplification of the decision-making process; thirdly, the reform of the constitutional system of the regions and local self-government, which significantly reduces the overlaps of competences by reducing the conflict produced by the reform of Title V; finally, the reform of the government system, which remained in an indefinite limbo. The virtues of perfect bicameralism, wanted by the constituent Assembly so that no one was completely excluded from the political direction, have transformed over time into evident criticalities. Two Houses that both have to vote for the government, with non-coincident electoral systems, active electoral and passive, differentiated, make any majority precarious. Almost a thousand Members who have to vote on identical legislative texts make the shuttles frequent and tiring, favouring the use of an unnerving obstruction that prevents the adoption of measures in physiological times. In this context, the reform we are discussing introduces a form of differentiated bicameralism in which the second chamber is assigned the essential function of representing the link with regional and territorial autonomy. If it gets to the bottom, it will still be a historic result. Having said that, and coming into line between institutional reform and electoral law, many in this House have also denounced the alleged autocratic nature of the reform. It has been argued that it would create an authoritarian system which gives the President of the Council master powers. Personally, I see no authoritarian drift, no lack of representation and no attempt to make the circuit opaque between sovereign determination of the people and the chosen model of the elect. It is said that a party could win the election with 30% and, after the victory at the ballot, control the only Legislative Chamber, however elected only in part with the vote of preference, without even having the counterweight of control of a strong Senate. So? In many other major democracies there are substantial majority prizes that lead to winning even those who have just taken 30% of the votes. It is a characteristic, this, which is found mainly in the French and British electoral systems which, in fact, have very substantial majority prizes. At the 2005 British politics, with 35.2 percent of the votes, Labour earned three hundred and fifty - six seats, with a 20 - percent prize. The same thing could be done for the French system. In many electoral systems, in short, the disproportion between voting percentages and assigned seats is very high, in the name of governability, but no one has ever questioned either the regularity of the vote or the full democratic nature of those systems. In addition, in seven European countries the second Chamber does not exist: in Finland, Denmark, Sweden, Greece, Luxembourg and Malta, Parliament is single-chamber. In the other eight countries only in Spain the second Chamber is largely elective. To find a high chamber with powers similar to our present Senate, one must go to the United States or Japan, while the European model is that of monocameralism or asymmetrical bicameralism. So, where would the scandal have been if you had chosen a Senate that was not directly elected by the citizens, if this is the rule in Europe? There has been a double agreement on the non-electivity of the Senate directly, but now the Democratic Party and the Government seem to have rethinked it and have considered to revise anyway the fifth paragraph of Article 2, with an amendment that actually overturns the approach followed so far and that speaks of "chosen in accordance with the choices expressed by the voters for the candidates advisors on the occasion of the renewal of the same organs," that is, of the regional councils. It is not, therefore, a second-level election endorsed by the voters, but a direct election of the new senators, who, having at this point the same legitimacy as the Members, could also aspire with some reason to give confidence to the Government. It would be like starting from the start. Let's hope not. However, it is important for us not to repeat the experiences of the past and not to fail this reform as well. Better a reform not perfect, if the alternative is to keep everything as it is now, in the belief that this legislature, out of an election without winners, makes sense only if it succeeds in being constituent. This reform provides for a strong reduction of parliamentary bicameralism and some strengthening of the government. This is what the centre-right - if I remember correctly - has been asking for for more than twenty years and, for consistency, I can only be in favour. Among other things, monocameralism and strengthening of the powers of the Government were also well present in the work of the Constituent. Calamandrei, Mortati and Perassi fought not to reach a parliamentary system with unstable governments: they knew that fascism was not the product of strong Executives, but of the weakness of the precarious governments of the liberal period. Today, in our political system, the instruments of pluralism and institutions designed to balance powers, to avoid excessive concentration in one body, have multiplied. Many powers have been referred to the European Union and the Regions, which act as counter-powers, conditioning and holding back the action of the Parliament-Government complex. Today, more than ever, we need executives who have a less fragile base and a longer life if we are to compete on equal terms in Europe. In a modern parliamentary democracy the balance of government powers is achieved through a strengthening of the opposition in the Chamber of Deputies, as well as the representation of regional governments in the Senate. It would therefore be more consistent to bind the vote of the senators to the indications of the Region that elected them, as happens - for example - in Germany, where in fact the members of the same Land are required to vote unitedly, on the basis of a predetermined choice of the institution they represent. This would be an antidote to the risk of the representatives of the new Senate attacking themselves according to party logics, typical of the Political Chambers, instead facilitating the expression of the variable territorial interests, according to what should be the authentic mission of the Federal Chambers. On the other hand, the first Italian regionalism has already been shipwrecked by the inability of the Regions to emancipate politically from the national organs of the political parties, which instead took place after the launch of constitutional law n. 1 of 1999, with the direct election of the Presidents of the Region, but giving rise to a situation in which the divergences of interests between the two territorial levels, national and regional, have almost never found adequate composition, giving rise to a considerable constitutional dispute aggravated then by the reform of Title V of 2001. Article 31 of the draft law in question contains the supremacy clause, which the State may exercise vis-à-vis the Regions. Therefore, the State will be able to deal with the competences of the Regions, allowing also the commissioning of Regions and local authorities in case of serious financial failure, and this goes in the right direction. But, in many cases, the border of competences remains too labile and uncertain. There is talk, in fact, of territorial planning within the competence of the Regions and of the government of the territory that belongs to the State: on this point there is a clear overlap of roles that will inevitably lead to new conflicts and a new series of appeals before the Constitutional Court. There is also one last critical issue that we have a duty to point out. Today we have three orders of legislative procedures: ordinary, constitutional legislation, decree-laws and delegated decrees. With the reform the legislative procedures become ten, too many: there is bicameral legislation, the monocameral one, the monocameral one with strengthened role of the Senate, that of the laws of popular initiative, etc. It is, therefore, ten different channels for the laws. If it is the Presidents of the Chamber and the Senate who will decide in agreement what is the pre-eminent matter, and therefore which channel will have to take the measure, it must be clearly written that the decisions of the Presidents of the Chamber and the Senate are indispensable in any place, otherwise again in this case new external conflicts are predictable. As for the functions of the new Senate, after the debauchery occurred in the passage to the Chamber, the forecast of the election of two constitutional judges by the Senate is reinstated: this is a fair index of the representative differentiation of the two Chambers. In fact, we will have three judges elected by the House, representative of the national community, and two judges elected by the Senate, representative of the autonomy. So the right balance returns. In conclusion, this reform, although with all the problems highlighted, represents a significant step forward to finally equip Italy with an institutional system comparable to that of the other major European democracies; a system which, by balance and restraint, is undoubtedly more solid than the one it replaces. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. She's registered to speak Senator De Petris. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u142	Madam President, in the democratic systems, the Constitutions lay down rules that should apply not for a few years, but for decades. And the validity and effectiveness of the democratic rate of those rules are measured not on the majorities, which inevitably are led to consider the immediate advantages, but on the minorities, who must feel guaranteed and participated in the pact that the Republic should hold for the next decades. In this case, Madam President - and I say this to my fellow Members, because everything that has happened in the various passages of the constitutional reform bill must always be reported - there has been a very opposite situation. Minorities have remained methodically uneducated, even when they made very serious and reasonable proposals, which certainly did not have the intention nor could be exchanged for attempts to sabotage an end in themselves, but aimed exclusively to render a true service to the Republic and, therefore, to make reform better, democratic and more effective; a constitutional reform that has been for years the mantra, a sort of obsession on which the various political ruling classes have measured themselves that, in these stormy years, have followed each other in our country. We have always thought that, in reality, the obsession of the reform was an alibi for those who were unable to govern the country, to give adequate answers to its real problems, almost blaming the Constitution for all their ineffective and inability. And, actually, the mantra has repeated itself. When I said that we have made an attempt, it is because - as the whole debate of the Constitutional Assembly teaches us - when the Constitution comes to fruition, we should come to a meeting between different and different political cultures and face the real problems facing us in the country, and for this reason I have spoken of unheard minorities. From the very first step, we have essentially put two questions: The real question we had to face was before everyone's eyes. I am referring to the crisis of representation and the real problem, which is repeated in every election, the disaffection of citizens and their lack of confidence in the institutions. Therefore, we needed to put in place more effective instruments to rebuild this pact, a trust. It is no coincidence that we have focused very much on the issue of enlargement of the instruments of participation: referendums, popular initiative laws and referendums. But in this case, too, we were unheard. Why is that? Constitutionalism was born to limit the sovereign, and not to give him more power. If anything, he was born to give more power to the people, and not to the only leader and sovereign. Instead, the opposite operation was carried out here. Secondly, we have said that we want to overcome the bicameralism of equality. There are serious and reasonable roads. We don't have to monkey, but we have to choose a path. As far as we are concerned, we have made a proposal - and I repeat it here - which was serious, reasonable and rational, which resulted in a reduction in the number of Members of the House, as in the Senate, and which attributed to the latter the powers of control. In all democratic systems, the real question is the question of controls and balances. On the other hand, the road that has been chosen is completely different, and not only because of the forcings, which we have also witnessed today. We are here in this House because in the Commission, after the declaration of inadmissibility and the withdrawal of the amendments (we have maintained only those of merit), we did not want to discuss the debate, which would have enabled it to remedy a number of obvious criticalities of this constitutional bill. Instead, what is the effect that has been produced? It's no use turning around. If we consider this reform and the electoral law (the Italicum) as a single integrated complex - and it is, of course, the objective is not to empower citizens anymore, to rebuild a relationship of trust, to restore efficiency and simplification to the democratic system. But, in reality, it is the modification of the form of government. There is no point in turning around: we are in fact changing the parliamentary system and the result of this affair is exactly overturning the balance between the powers that have been defined by the Constitution, in order to assign to the legislative power, that is to say to Parliament, a function that is only ancillary to the Commission. This is what is produced with Italicum. And the problem is the majority prize and the ballot, which will have a distorting effect on the representation system. In addition to all this, the path chosen for overcoming the perfect bicameralism is a sort of senate larva, whose functions, among other things, after the passage to the House, are absolutely messy (there is no proper function), and without having any more its popular legitimacy. We will have to discuss this in Parliament; the issue is not closed - I say this to the Minister too - with the agreement made within the Democratic Party. That proposal does not solve the fundamental issue that many constitutionalists have pointed out to us. There are byzantineisms and escamotage to bypass, to give a little more indication by the citizens. But, in reality, we are always faced with an indirect election and the fact that there is no more popular legitimacy. When there is the legislative function - and this "Senaticchio," although modified, a legislative function still has it, since it retains competence on the constitutional revision - it can only come from the people, only if there is direct suffrage and popular legitimacy. This is a basic democratic principle. When we talk - for example - about the French Senate, the confrontation does not stand. Meanwhile, that Senate is elected by 150,000 great voters. But, in Article 3 of the French Constitution, there is precisely the explicit declination of representation, as direct and indirect election. This isn't our case. Article 1 of our Constitution, which I have quoted several times and which the Constitutional Court itself refers to in its ruling on Porcellum, is violated. And that is the fundamental point. All political bodies that carry out legally binding deliberations for the whole community must find their legitimacy in the popular vote. The draft agreement does not solve this and it also creates a problem - I mean - because the issue of the transitional rule in Article 39 is not addressed. It will then happen that perhaps there will be about twenty senators elected perhaps among the advisers, in accordance with the electoral instructions, although I do not know how this will happen; but the other 55, because of that transitional rule, will be chosen from among the regional advisers. Even if that amendment were passed, only by 2020 would we have a Senate in line with that plant. The underlying ideology, in fact, is to subtract any relationship and legitimacy from the citizens' vote in the end. I have the impression that, precisely in order not to deal with this, there is a risk of creating further problems, from a technical and democratic point of view. All in all - I repeat - there are no balances, there is no adequate system of control, while democratic systems are made of this. Everything is unbalanced in favour of the Executive and a single party. We will have as effect that there will be a leader, other than the imitation of the sovereign and the king: it will be the sovereign who chooses those who will have to stay in Parliament and who will reduce Parliament to an ancillary function. The problem of balances, therefore, is very serious. As far as we are concerned, as always - and that is why I wanted to remind you of the passages in which we presented our proposals (a hundred and fifty senators, three hundred and fifty Members), in which we have dealt with various issues concerning very different functions, in a very clear way, out of the mess that has been created - we would like to be on the subject. We endured forcing at first reading, forcing the House, forcing last week to bring the measure to the House, so we cannot now be held responsible. In any case, we do not think - at least you owe this to this House - that this is all over: we do not make the Constitution only in a party or in a party direction. We are therefore always prepared, as we have already done in the Commission, to keep all the substantive amendments - only the substantive ones - to address the real issues that exist and there are many open ones. I do not have time to say here - and we will then address the issue - what this kind of Senate will mean with regard to the election of the President of the Republic, nor to focus on the various issues of rotation: there will never be a plenum from this point of view, because the regional councils change, there are elections. In addition, there are the problems of the guarantee bodies, guarantees, controls and balances, because this is what democratic constitutions are made of. On the other hand, we would not like to see our country's transition from parliamentary democracy to what some people call 'decision-making democracy', which is so exalted, the 'democracy of investiture', as I also like to say, in the sense that the citizens remain only to say who is the leader. And look, in a few years, we'll touch the devastating effects with our hands. That is all we want to say, so that someone can say that at least we have opposed each other, with dignity and pride, to defend our Constitutional Charter and the essence of constitutional principles. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u143	He's enlisted to speak Senator Gualdani. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u144	Madam President, representatives of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, the reform debate on Part II of the Constitution has been going on for many years and the constitutionalists have long agreed on the need to overcome the perfect bicameralism in order to ensure a more streamlined procedure that does not extend to the decision-making techniques that often constitute obstacles to efficient responses to the real needs of the country. We have the extremely heavy task of seeking a solution, which is as far-sighted as possible, as should any response given by a Parliament in the process of modernising the rules on which the democratic game must be based in the coming years. I hope that in the search for this solution the oppositions can be constructively involved to improve some critical points raised and of vital importance. It certainly does not benefit the monstrous amount of amendments aimed at ostracizing any attempt to modernize for the preservation of old corporate logic. It is necessary until the end, Madam Minister, to find an agreement that involves the entire parliamentary arc in order to write together the fundamental norms of our Constitutional Charter. The game is basically played all around the question of the electiveity of the senators. Although the reform provides for the lack of trust between the Senate and the Executive, it would be essential to ensure, in indirect form, a form of popular election of senators, especially at a time when the senators themselves contribute to the legislative function, which is also constitutional in nature. And we are ready, as a whole Group of the People's Area, to seek a solution that is widely shared by the entire Parliament and the citizens. It cannot be denied that the best aspect of constitutional reform is the modification of Title V, which, since 2001, in its present form, has caused many problems: from the overlap of competences between the State and the Regions, to the greater complexity of the legal framework at national level in the areas subject to shared competence. This confusing regulatory framework has generated above all poor attractiveness of our country to investments, removing any possibility of development even in key sectors for the peninsula economy. Hence the need to eliminate the shared power, simplifying the relationship between the State and the Regions and avoiding the continuous recourse to the Constitutional Court. As far as the powers of the Senate are concerned, when we are debating the amendments, we should be very clear on what functions to assign to the Senate. If it is to be a Chamber of Representation of the Regions, it is impossible not to give it the role of liaison between the Regions and the European Union and between the State and the European Union itself. A quality assurance role should also be specified. The Senate should become the House in which public policies are assessed: that is, it should be equipped with a structure capable of measuring the effects of the actions of the public decision-makers, the results of the rules in their concrete implementation, in order to ensure that all the necessary corrective improvements can be made subsequently to avoid the unexpected effects of the public policy-makers. The Senate is and must remain an institution of excellence, not deprived of its extraordinary human resources. It is therefore also necessary to assess the extent to which it is involved in the legislative process. To create a plurality of procedures depending on the subject could become dangerous for the unclear limitation of the same. Therefore, in addition to the specific cases in which it exercises a shared legislative competence, it is necessary to clarify once and for all the incisiveness of the Senate in the procedure, through the different types of opinions that the same may and will have to issue on a measure. Otherwise, there is a risk of making even more cumbersome a process that actually needs to be streamlined and simplified. On the other hand, with regard to the role of the Commission, the possibility of requesting the approval of draft laws deemed essential for the implementation of the government programme and the codification in Constitution of the criteria emerged in jurisprudence for the adoption and conversion of decree-laws. In addition, the text now in the Senate for a new reading includes among its salient points the ones I am going to list. The preventive judgment of legitimacy on electoral laws, according to which the laws governing the election of members of the Chamber of Deputies and of the Senate of the Republic may be submitted, before their promulgation, to the preventive judgment of constitutional legitimacy by the Constitutional Court on a reasoned appeal lodged by at least a quarter of the members of the Chamber of Deputies or a third of the members of the Senate within ten days of the approval of the law. Until the decision of the Constitutional Court the deadline for the enactment of the law remains suspended. In the event of a declaration of constitutional illegitimacy, the law cannot be promulgated. Moreover, there is a strong protection of the rights of parliamentary minorities in the text. The Chamber Regulations guarantee the rights of parliamentary minorities. The Rules of Procedure of the Chamber of Deputies govern the statute of opposition. Another salient point of the reform is the constitutionalisation of a proactive referendum. In order to encourage the participation of citizens in the determination of public policies, the proposed and guiding popular referendums are introduced, as well as other forms of consultation, including social formations, the conditions and effects of which are laid down by a constitutional law. Article 99 of the Constitution currently governing the composition and functions of the National Economic and Labour Council is also deleted. In addition, all the norms relating to the Provinces are expelled from the text of Title V, bringing to an end the process of remodeling the constituent institutions of the Republic that began with the Delrio law last year. With regard to regional differentiated autonomy, the Regions will be able to see themselves devolved by the State "further forms and special conditions of autonomy" on justice of peace, education, active labour policies, vocational education and training, cultural goods and tourism, government of the territory, but on conditions that they are "in equilibrium between income and expenditure." In order to curb the indiscriminate expenditure of politics and to give a moral response to citizens, limits to the costs of regional policy are constitutionalised. A law of the Republic will determine the amounts payable to the presidents of the Regions and to the members of the regional bodies, which may not in any case exceed those payable to the mayors of the municipalities capital of the Region. Monetary transfers, which bear public finance charges, are prohibited in favour of the political groups of the Regional Councils. Further progress towards the full affirmation of gender equality is the prediction that the laws laying down the procedures for the election of the Chambers promote the balance between women and men in representation in Parliament. Clearly, of all the aspects that have now been addressed, there is certainly no denying perfectibility, but not the possibility of turning over a plant whose format has already been widely shared by this House and then by the House of Deputies. It is up to us in the next few days to guarantee the Senate exclusively the dignity of the High House, as it is. And give the feeling back to the citizens that this country is not at the mercy of defeatism and the inability to renew itself, but that it can give the appropriate answers to face the challenges that the future holds us. [[Applause from the AP Group (NCD-UDC)]]

ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u148	Madam President, representatives of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, we have a lot of eyes on us, on this House. By virtue of this reform, thanks to that flexibility clause in Europe that this Government has longed for, we will also be able to obtain additional resources to be used in the law of stability for investments for new industrial policies and for a universal measure against poverty, in short, to ensure that many manage to reach their feet at the end of a crisis. We know that this crisis is very hard: we have been living it for too long, it has jammed our democracy, it has struck social groups that are the heart of our society, removing protagonism and opportunities, relegating vital energies to the margins. Our task is to put them in a position to get back up and be able to make them do to those who are excluded today, because when a boy renounces to study it is very difficult for him to find the strength to start again; when he closes the door of a factory, it is very difficult for him to reopen. There is a very close link between the institutional crisis and the social crisis: if we do not want resentment to overwhelm the institutions, we must change radically and courageously, giving not only a voice, but also a chance to affect those who do not have it today. To do this, we need a strong, autonomous, effective and capable policy, because politics, if it is weak, is overwhelmed by the strength of finance and the economy; if it decides to remain weak, as it has been in recent years, it betrays itself, its mandate, the trust that has been given to us, which is the most precious asset that we have. In fact, inefficient institutions are not able to heal our evils, the social fractures, the territorial ones, the inequalities that block the country. We are dealing not only with ourselves, but all of this House, a test of maturity and credibility. Are we able to make reforms? Are we able to change the country or will we stop again? Once again we will give up, will we be enemies of ourselves and Italy? Here, for us democrats, this reform means bringing about a project of change that is not born in these months, but is born more than twenty years ago with the Olive tree: a pact between politics and citizens to rewrite the Common Charter of the Republic in which to recognize itself. This is a reform that wants to secure and give new life to a parliamentarism that today is first of all hostage to its degenerations, inconclusions, paralysis, long delays, that we try to circumvent by resorting to confidence, to maximaments, to decrees of urgency, to delegated laws, too many times ending not with solving but with aggravating the crisis. Parliamentarianism, not only the parties, is today under attack by populism and by an insurgent nationalism; a danger that we thought defeated forever, and that instead re-emerges today. This was also the case in the 1930s, and we know how it ended: liberal democracies were overwhelmed by authoritarianism because they were weak, because they were unable to cope with the crisis. That is why it strikes me that in the debate on the reforms of recent months there has been a great deal of insistence on the lack of counterweights and guarantees in the relationship between the Government and Parliament, when we know that this is not the case. The biggest problem we have, the weak link of our institutional pluralism, is today the lack of stability of the government: this is the guarantee that is lacking and that serves our democracy. The reforms we are making are anchoring our system to a parliamentary government to be strengthened. This has reminded us in a very strong way, in a beautiful speech in the Committee on Institutional Affairs of the Senate, President Giorgio Napolitano; a speech in which they echoed the recent words of Leopoldo Elia, those that date back to the Constitution of Calamandrei. Here, the strengthening of the parliamentary government, and not the introduction of semi-presidency or strong premiered: formulas on which, even in the past, the center-left has also questioned. For this reason I am struck by the accusation that is being made to these reforms of authoritarianism, to the binomial between new electoral law and Senate reform: an unfounded and harmful accusation, first of all because Italicum restores legitimacy after ten years of Porcellum: restores colleges, and it will not be a law that will have more majority effects than those that had, for example, the so-called Mattarellum. This wrong assumption has led to an equally wrong request in the debate of these weeks: the direct election of the members of the Senate identical to that of the Members of Parliament. We cannot say, in fact, that we want to overcome the bicameralism of equality, to want two different Chambers, a politics and one representing the territorial institutions, and then to ask that the two compositions be equal, that they have the same legitimacy, that they are both an expression of the same popular will. They are terms in contradiction with each other and in contradiction with the spirit of a reform that provides that the Senate does not give confidence to the Government, which represents the autonomy, and for this reason it is a great and important innovation: because it restores function to municipalism, and above all to a regionalism that is experiencing a very serious crisis in recent years. Because it fulfils the wishes of the debate held in the constituent assembly; it responds to the changes and social changes of recent years and to the modification of Title V of Part II of the Constitution, avoiding numerous disputes between the State and the regions that have been there in the past; it responds to the need for local self-governments to participate in the construction of Community policies and in the elaboration of European law. It is good that a Senate of the autonomous bodies representing the territorial authorities is composed of advisers who are at the same time senators, chosen with a second degree election according to the indications of the citizens. It is good that this is written in the amendments tabled this morning by President Finocchiaro, which strengthen the dictates of the reform. The Democratic Party and the majority of the government will be united in this House, where I hope there will be a larger majority, as always in the area of reform. We will be united in the referendum that will take place in 2016, so that such an important reform will have a shared and broad consensus and will not be relegated to areas that are often indecipherable, such as the House or the talk shows. This government was born to make reforms and break a spell that every time prevented them. This is his strength, but if not, his irrecoverable weakness too. We are demonstrating that this legislature, which seemed to be lost and born under bad auspices, can really be a constituent and mark a historical watershed. In this House we are demonstrating that we are working to put a jammed car back in motion, while there are those who, Senator Calderoli, mock this House and our fellow citizens by tabling 82 million amendments. ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u154	Madam President, my role as Senator Secretary of Parliament has imposed some discipline on me in the presence of the House during these four days of debate, which I have heard almost entirely. So it came to my mind a phrase that one of my teachers, one of the great rectors of the Catholic University of Milan, Giuseppe Lazzati - who was also an important constituent Father, having been part of the constituent Assembly - loved to repeat as a compass, for anyone who wanted to engage in reforms and change. He referred both to reforms within the church and, of course, to reforms in civil society, institutions and politics. It was a phrase of Saint Ambrose, the great founding bishop of the diocese of Milan, who said: "Nova semper quaerere et parta guardare". At the same time, we must desire, ask, want intensely, new things, change, without being afraid of them, and, at the same time, leave to guard, that is, to guard carefully, almost with jealousy, the great things that we have received from the past. I believe that when you approach something as important as the Constitution you have to have this double feeling: one verse must not be afraid of change, rather search for it, look for the new, because history walks and you have to walk with history and sometimes even chase it and, on the other, you have to guard the important things of the past. If, therefore, we are trying to see, at the heart, the meaning of this reform of ours, it is because we are looking for innovation and at the same time want to preserve something important. The important thing we want to preserve from the second part of the Constitution - the first is not in question - is the form of parliamentary government. After all this is also the red thread that accompanies the action of the center-left Italian, from the years of the Olive tree to today. We have sometimes defended, even harshly, particularly in the confrontation with the centre-right, the form of parliamentary government, which the constituents have given us. This is the "parta" that we want to guard. [[Senator Taverna's comments.]] LUCIDES (M5S). We wouldn't miss that, too! [[Call the president back.]] There are other systems which, legitimately, are based on other ideas: there is a form of presidential or semi-presidency government, which has its merits and which is certainly in the core of democratic systems. We have defended, and we also do so with the reform under consideration, the form of parliamentary government, that is, the idea that the Government must be an expression of Parliament and must find in Parliament the moment of its constitutional legitimacy, the place where it finds confidence, which knows that it can be revoked at any time.
