ParlaMint-IT_2015-09-23-LEG17-Senato-sed-510.ana.u158	TONINI (PD). I listened for four days in a row and would like to have my ten minutes to say mine. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. That's right. You are allowed, as others have been allowed. TONINI (PD). Many of the interventions of the senators that I have listened to patiently and diligently posed the following problem: this government is not legitimized directly by the popular vote. They are right, but at the same time they say a blasphemy from the constitutional point of view, because from the point of view of the 1948 Constitution, no government is elected by voters, as governments find their legitimacy in the trust of Parliament. However, it is true and sacred that in our time there is a desire for direct legitimization of governments by citizens and voters. In fact, we all say that Tsipras was elected, but the other day Tsipras was elected only as a Member, but the President of the Greek Republic would never have dreamed of giving office to a different personality, because Tsipras won the election by reaching 34-35 per cent and, thanks to the majority prize that also provides for that system, reaches the absolute majority of the seats. [[Applause from the PD Group]] Let's say that Cameron governs the United Kingdom, he is British Prime Minister, because the British wanted him; of course, the British elected him a member of his college, but it is certain that Cameron, being the leader of the Conservative Party that won the election, governs Great Britain very legitimately. Mrs Merkel is not directly elected by the Germans, but by the Bundestag; however, we all say that she is an expression of the German people. NUGNES (M5S). Who says that? TONINI (PD). In 2013 we did not have a government elected by the citizens, legitimized directly by the citizens, because our system, as it is designed today, while it is able to resolve and preserve what needs to be preserved, that is, the parliamentary form, is not able to deal with this new question of direct legitimacy that there is from the citizens. This is the contradiction in which we live. The reform we are debating is intended to dissolve this contradiction, because one way we are maintaining the system of parliamentary democracy, given that the President of the Council whom we will have with the reform will still need the confidence of the House; he will have no new power compared to what the Constitution assigns to the President of the Council today. At the same time, the combined arrangement of reform of bicameralism (so overcoming the bicameralism of equality and the need that the Senate also gives confidence) and majority electoral law will allow citizens to be their protagonists of the legitimacy of the government. [[Applause from the PD Group]] This is the synthesis that we are trying to build. Of course, this summary may not be shared; this is entirely legitimate, because it is an absolutely controversial issue. However, this choice, the choice of this synthesis, this mediation and - let me also say - this compromise between both fundamental values (the centrality of Parliament and the need for clear popular legitimacy), this synthesis, this mediation, this compromise is the prevailing model in Europe, is the way in which European democracies work. Each in its own way, but all are variations on this theme, unique and fundamental. There are some exceptions, for example the French system, which is absolutely part of the democracies. We in the centre-left have long chosen to be in this fundamental current of European thinking and of the European political system. We have also applied this compromise to the election of senators. If you think about it, we did the same thing, through a debate even bitter among ourselves, in which we were able to listen to each other, also to learn something from each other and in the end we arrived at a compromise between two fundamental values for the Senate. For one verse we chose a model of the Senate. We could also choose the monocameral model and suppress the Senate; I heard many people say that rather then it would have been better to close the Senate. Rather than synthesizing, it is better to take the extreme route. No, we have considered that all the great European nations are bicameral nations, which are based on a bicameral Parliament; we want to overcome the bicameralism of equality, but we want to be within the family of bicameral systems. The second Chamber must therefore have a precise function, if it wants to be something useful and important. This specific function is to be the chamber of representation of territorial institutions. If it wants to be a Chamber of representation of territorial institutions and bring the regional legislator to a direct confrontation in Parliament with the national legislator, it must be elected by the regional councils. In fact, if it does not have the legitimacy of who is elected by citizens to govern the Regions, how can it represent the Regions themselves? This is a real thing, it's the truth that was inside the draft that got here. However, some of our colleagues have raised an equally real problem: be careful not to close the issue in the Chamber of the Regional Council, where there are certainly legitimate people, who, however, could resolve the issue of representation in Parliament in an internal mediation, which excludes or at least appears to exclude citizens. That is the compromise that has been found. Like the British Prime Minister or the German Chancellor or the Head of the Greek Government or tomorrow, the President of the Italian Council will not be directly elected by the people, but will have a clear legitimacy through a system that produces governability and produces a majority, just as the senators will not be directly elected by the citizens, but will be elected by the regional councils through a system that will give them a full democratic legitimacy. [[Senator Santangelo's comments.]] So we will have the advantage of having senators who will have two legitimacys behind them together, that of the people who chose them and that of the Regional Council that elected them. I believe that this is the way of nova semper quaerere et parta guardare. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. I invite you to conclude, Senator Tonini. TONINI (PD). I shall conclude with one final point. We had a great challenge ahead of us and I think we were able to face it. We now have a tough and tough debate ahead of us in the next few days. I believe that if we can see in our debate this basic idea that we have before us, we will be able to give our debate a constructive and proactive turn, able to bring our citizens back, as we have to do, to our institutions. [[Applause from the PD Group and the Senators Chiavaroli and Barani. Congratulations.]]
